overo verticuli di pretiose petre pertusati, traducto per gli quali filamento aureo, et omni cosa requisitamente cum iucundissimo coloramento depicturata, et cum scintillante illustratione splendicante, et le gesticulatrice inchirotecate di chiroteche cum acuale textura in multiplici noduli et floruli di filatura aurea et argentea, et di seta di vario coloramine artificiosamente contexte, cum crumenale conclusura in uno phrygiolo dialitho agli rosei, et carnosi brachii, cum cordicule auree confuse venustamente di varia coloratione di seta elegante congeste. Praeiva tutte queste trophigere una evulsa et exprompta, la vexillatione dilla navicula gestatrice cum prompta gerulatione procurreva. Et immediate imitaria sequiva un’altra, cum una triumphale celibari, la quale nel supremo haveva uno alato Cupidine et disvelaminato in acto cum l’archo pectente calcava uno pomo, nel supremo inflexo d’una gioia di foliatione laurina di bractea. La quale era supra uno fundo inverso di gutturnio vaso. Et quivi illaqueata era, le fasceole lambente di supra lo interstitio dilla gioia extra burrivano volante. Intro il contento dil circulo era una tabella, per la crassitudine dilla quale la hasta terebrava, et per una pila, alla tabella, nella mediana parte extrinseca superassidente, et cusì nella sua parte ima un’altra subiacente penetrava. Similmente nello abacato di ambidui et laevo et dextro lato prosiliva alquanto di hastula transacto per la gioia, et fora promineva. Dall’uno et dal altro prominente pendici erano dui funiculi intorti di oro et di serico, per bache di pretiose petre traiectato. Sotto il rivexo dilla gioia, uno gutturnio vaso cum il fundo supernate subigeva, et lo orificio in balaustica apertione, una figura ovolata mordicava cum liniamenti imitanti la forma. Cum dui pomuli supra la circumferentia uno per lato, nel medio diametrale, nel imo un’altra pila. Postremamente uno nextrulo aureo cum variata textura volitante. Nella tabella da una et l’altra facie era di maiuscule graece tale titulo. DORYKTETOI.

— or little whorls of precious stones, pierced, through which was drawn a golden filament; and every thing requisitely depicted with most pleasant colouring, and shining with a scintillating illumination. And the gesticulating dancers gloved with gloves of needle- texture, in manifold little knots and florets of gold and silver thread, and of silk of various colouring artfully interwoven, with a purse-like closure in a jewelled phrygium (embroidered band) at the rosy and fleshy arms, with little gold cords beautifully mingled, elegantly heaped, of various colouring of elegant silk.

Before all these trophy-bearers ran an outstretched and displayed one: the standard-bearing of the little boat's conveyance ran ahead with prompt bearing. And immediately, in imitation, followed another, with a triumphal celibar (standard), which at the top had a winged Cupid, unveiled, in the act of drawing with the bow, treading upon an apple; at the top, inflected, a jewel of laurel-foliage of foil. Which was upon an inverted bottom of an ewer-vase. And there it was ensnared: the little bands, licking above the interval of the jewel, burst out flying. Within the content of the circle was a tablet, through the thickness of which the spear bored, and through a sphere superimposed on the tablet in the middle of the outer part; and so in its lowest part another, subjacent, penetrated. Likewise in the abacus-work, on both the left and right side, a little spear-shaft jutted somewhat, passed through the jewel, and stood forth outside. From the one and the other jutting pendant were two little cords twisted of gold and silk, threaded through berries of precious stones. Under the reverse of the jewel, an ewer-vase with its bottom uppermost pressed on; and its orifice, in a balustered opening, bit an egg-shaped figure, with lineaments imitating the form. With two little apples above the circumference, one to a side, in the middle of the diameter; at the bottom another sphere. Lastly a golden little knot, with varied texture, fluttering. On the tablet, on the one and the other face, was, in Greek majuscules, this title:

ΔΟΡΥΚΤΗΤΟΙ (DORYKTETOI)
— "The spear-won" (captives taken by the spear).

Notes

All secure; the standard's tiered ornament is described in exhaustive goldsmith's detail.

<!--[Iscrizione nella figura:] DORYKTETOI-->

Et molti altri falerati cum molto polimine di varii flori, fronde, et fructuli, non sencia sotiatione di bractee d’oro, et argento, et di omni coloramento enchausticamente infecte, cum infiniti ornamini et exquisita elegantia di politura, progrediente laetissime, cum altre assai insigniture et victoriale notule, et di exuvie et spolie et manubii trophaeali decoramenti, et dal potente et divino fanciullo sagittabondo, devicte praecedente cum moderata distantia di triumphale processo, et cum molto plauso et divo thiaso, et cum soni et gutturiali iubili dille pheretrie gestante. Daposcia prima apresentatovise la sua diva uxore Psyche, cum regilla veste, cum fulcimento villoso, et chermeo oro collustrante di textura aurea triliciata et tramata, di lucente serico induta. Quale Hermaria et Malicha di Hesperia non produceno. Et subsequente le comite di sericie veste di discreta et versicolore infectura laute et genialemente indute, cum habiti superbi invisitati et novi, opulentissimamente dialithi. Quali la natura nostra ad gli humani ingegni novercamente fare interdice, cum crispule pliche rugosamente sopra gli delicati et quam mundissimi corpusculi, et sopra le verginale anche, al suave impulso dille fresche aure inquietule. Alcune cum inhaerenti pectorali di squamea operatura aurea, cum venusto et sotiale ornato di lucentissime gemme. Et altre gli albissimi pecti, più che le hyberne pruine di Capricorno inducti haveano gli assettati vestimenti, cum decoramento et delicie dil primo tuberare dille indomite mammillule, quale omelli orthomastici cum semiglomulo porrecte et affixe, sopra di esse fina al pectorale cincto in colore cyaneo depilo incochleava uno sumptuoso phrygio suffarcinato di orientale perle, alquanto tuberulante occupava dapaticamente tutta la diaphragma, overo tutta quella parte supra il cinctorio di artificiosi voluti, di egregia foliatura, quale nunque in Phrygia dagli Phrygioni fue invento. Et in colore phoeniceo, aliquantulo quella incochleatura di vernanti x ii

And many others, trapped-out with much polish of various flowers, fronds, and little fruits, not without the company of gold and silver foil, and infected with every colouring encaustically, with infinite ornaments and exquisite elegance of polish — proceeding most joyously, with many other insignia and victory-marks, and with trophy-ornaments of exuviae and spoils and plunder, conquered by the potent and divine arrow-shooting boy, preceding with moderate distance of triumphal procession, and with much applause and divine revel-rout, and with sounds and guttural jubilees of the quiver-bearers.

Then, first, there presented herself his goddess-wife Psyche, in a queenly robe, with a villous underlay, and shining with crimson gold, of gold texture triple-twilled and wefted, clad in lucent silk — such as Hermaria and Malicha of Hesperia do not produce. And, in sequence, the companions in silken robes of discreet and versicolour dye, sumptuously and genially clad, with superb habits, unwonted and new, most opulently jewelled — such as our nature interdicts the human wit to make, step-motherly — with crisp pleats wrinkling over the delicate and most-clean little bodies, and over the virginal hips, at the soft impulse of the fresh, restless breezes.

Some with adhering breastplates of scaled gold-work, with comely and companionable adornment of most-lucent gems. And others had set their fitted garments over the whitest breasts, whiter than the winter frosts of Capricorn, with adornment and delight at the first swelling of the untamed little breasts — like little rounded apples, upright-nippled, with a half-cluster put forth and affixed — and above them, up to the breast-band, in a cyanean depilate colour, a sumptuous phrygium wound spiralling, stuffed with oriental pearls, somewhat swelling, occupying bounteously the whole diaphragm, or all that part above the girdle, of artful volutes, of egregious foliage, such as was never invented in Phrygia by the Phrygian embroiderers. And in a Phoenician (crimson) colour, that spiralling somewhat of springing —

smaragdi pillulati promineva. Et in colore praxino, di ardenti rubini baccati. Et nelle veste crocotule di illustranti saphyri spondilati, et in colore purpureo, overo luculeo chermeso di praelucentissimi adamanti pyramidali. In tinctura ametistina, overo punicea di iuste et candidissime margarite. Et cusì erano et ancora cum questa dispensatione altri conspicui et vivissimi coloramenti, quale il molochino, thyreo, muriceo, sandaraceo ferrugineo, et ballaustico. Gli quali vestimenti di seta tonsi erano alcuni, altri villosi, et alcuni in triplici et quadruplici graduli di villatura, cum opera in gratissime figure di flori et animaluli excellentemente texti. Altre cum panno sericeo di ordimento di seta et trama d’oro et argento in omni coloratione et figuratione, cum summa disquisitione di arte contexti. Alcune cum zonate vestiture alternatamente, modo una aurea, modo cyanea, poscia viride, et consequente argentea, et cum reliqui coloramenti cum amicale et correlario distributo, non sencia venusta textura, et voluptica obiectatione. Alcune havevano ostrine investiture, et bibaphe tyrie, et altre gli amicti epicroci, quale mai in Scythia di l’arborea lanugine se feceron diversamente segmentate et variegate. Altre cum tenue palio bombicino inumbrava el spectabile alvo presulamente adhaerendo. Accessoriamente negli comosi capiti di epse ornaticulamente nymphei baltei cum mira congruentia gemmati, vermiculariamente in exquisitissimi retioli de invento eximie operati, et cum vitte cum filamine aureo riquadratule in figura rhombea et reticulate, et cum auree tiare contecti, tanto che gli intorquati fili et congeminati erano distincti l’uno dal altro, cum triplice assotiatione, uno aureo ritortulo supra dui sericei prominente, cum exquisita nodulatura ove obviavasi. Tra gli quali le circulare et splendescente gemme ordinate variamente sutte erano secundo che degli vestimenti la varietate è dicto. Altre di diadema aurea el capo divamente stringente. Negli rhombiculi albicavano, (et similmente in tutte) rose facte di sei crassi unione, dille quale in medio prominente in forma conoida resplendeva uno crasso gioiello dilla sortitione che ambiva per la vitta imphrygiata. Etiam dalla parte che sepiva per sopra il spatioso fronte cum arsineo ornamento. Ove il discrime degli uberrimi capilli dividevase, cum venusto vestigio di summisse undicule. Altre elegantemente havevano adtexti al capo gli aurei crini, cum geniale glomulo. Alcuni cum torti noduli cum stricto tumore bellatulamente concinnata la formosa cervice in grumulo nympheaticamente collecti et revincti et cum cordiculi di seta et d’oro intorqueatuli. Altre havevano il tenuissimo capillamento sopra le piane tempore ornatissimamente innexo. Et tale poscia parte dilla

— of little emeralds in pellets stood forth. And in leek-green colour, of ardent rubies in berries. And on the saffron robes, of shining sapphires in whorls; and in purple, or bright crimson colour, of most-lucent pyramidal diamonds. In amethystine tincture, or Punic (red), of just and whitest pearls.

And thus there were, and again with this dispensation, other conspicuous and most-vivid colourings — such as mallow, thyreus, murex-purple, sandarac, iron-grey, and pomegranate- flower. Which silken garments were some shorn, others villous, and some in triple and quadruple little gradations of pile, with work in most-pleasing figures of flowers and little animals excellently woven. Others with silken cloth of silk warp and weft of gold and silver in every colouring and figuring, interwoven with the utmost disquisition of art. Some with zoned vestments alternately — now a gold, now a cyan, then green, and consequent silver — and with the remaining colourings distributed amicably and correlatively, not without comely texture, and voluptuous show. Some had oyster-purple investitures, and twice-dipped Tyrian; and others the saffron-mantles, such as were never in Scythia made, of the arboreal down (cotton), diversely segmented and variegated. Others, with a thin cotton pall, shadowed the noteworthy belly, closely adhering.

Accessorily, on their tufted heads, ornamental nymphish fillets, gemmed with wondrous congruity, wormwise worked in most-exquisite little nets of choice invention; and with ribbons with golden filament squared in rhombic figure and reticulated, and covered with golden tiaras — so that the twisted and doubled threads were distinct one from another, with a triple association, one gold twist standing forth above two silken, with exquisite little knotting where they met. Among which the circular and resplendent gems were variously sewn in order, according as the variety of the garments is told. Others, a golden diadem divinely binding the head.

In the little rhombs whitened (and likewise in all) roses made of six thick pearls-in-union, of which, standing forth in the middle in conoid form, resplended a thick jewel of the allotment that went round by the embroidered fillet. Also from the part that hedged over the spacious forehead with royal ornament, where the parting of the richest hair divided itself, with the comely trace of low little waves. Others elegantly had woven to the head their golden locks, with a genial little cluster —

festevola fronte occupava venustamente tenivano di capillamento circinato intorto et concrispato, como illustri fili aurei, et cum angustuli circumvoluti capreolati et ad alcune la rosea fronte contegevano le anteventule comule, et alle moderate aure delitiosamente inconstantule, decorosamente al capo et al collo circumiacenti. Et retro ancora per medio le bellissime orechie, la extrema parte dille vitte imphrygiata di aequati rubini corruscanti, et tra gli quali cum egregia copula di adamanti et di smeragdi vernanti, et cyanissimi saphiri, erano dependuli multiplici straleti di bractea d’oro et argentea instabili scintillanti, subtensa sotto le trece, cum una infilatura di orientale perle. Il residuo d’indi dilla copiosa et promissa capillatura cum vagissime undule confluctuante commenso dalle delicatissime spalle fino ultra le rotonde sure di sopra via dalle dure et freschissime nate effusissimo vagante discorreva. Alcune lascivamente gli mundissimi capilli dalla ornatissima cervice demissi, et dall’ocipitio dependuli, cum aequale discrepantia da uno illigamento d’oro, de qui et de lì dividentisi, sopra il lacteo collo. Daposcia bellamente, et cum eximia politione in egregio vortice sinuati et conglobati, convenivano elegantemente al summo vertice, in acumine extenuati ove uno pretiosissimo flocco di perle ad summa voluptate ambe due le treciature stringeva, di sotto via le picole aure maestrevolmente conducte, traiectate sopra le piane tempore, et pervenivano, cum aequa extenuatione alla sua residentia et voluptico convento. Ad alcune erano subrutuli gli capilli, cum spectatissimi involvuli circum il capo cingente cum amicale floramine vernante. Alcuni flavi più che electo arsinico folioso di lustro praestanti, et di colore, cum decoro vertigine capreolatamente compositi al nympheo et voluptico exornato cum bellatula adiunctione di varii et pretiosi lapilli, et ad expresso di extrema delitia, alcuni nigelli più che le piume anthracine di Esacho, in venusto volumine collustranti di limpidissimi velamini aurei contecti, et sopra il discrime, cum achi crinali detenti, cum conspicuo et gratioso volato, cum multiplici innodamenti cincinnati, cum multa splendescentia, cum arte et artificio, che omni lascivo studio et voluptico nymphale excedeva, comptamente pectinati, et decorissimamente compositi. Laqueoli inescati di retinente, mordace, et noxio visco degli amorosi cori. Cum spire di valore inextimabile alle pertusate aure suspese, et cum pretiosissimi monili et segmenti torquata et cincta la dricta et nivente gula, cum facinorosi collarii sumptuosamente ingemmati, cum più excellente conquisito di exornato et in decorare tanto affecto muliebre, che mai si potesse subtilmente excogitare.

— some with twisted little knots, with a tight swelling prettily arranged, the shapely nape gathered nymph-wise into a little clump, and bound again with little cords of silk and gold, twisted. Others had the most-tenuous hair over the flat temples most ornately woven in. And some then held part of the festive forehead comely-occupied with hair curled, twisted and crisped, like illustrious golden threads, and with narrow tendril-like curls circling round; and, for some, the fore-blowing little locks covered the rosy forehead, and, at the moderate breezes delightfully inconstant, lay decorously about the head and neck. And behind, too, through the middle of the most-beautiful ears, the outermost part of the embroidered fillet, of even rubies coruscating, and among them, with egregious coupling of diamonds and of springing emeralds and most-cyan sapphires, hung manifold little darts of gold and silver foil, unstable, scintillating, stretched beneath the tresses, with a threading of oriental pearls. The rest, thence, of the copious and abundant hair, fluctuating with the vaguest little waves, measured from the most-delicate shoulders down past the round calves, above and down from the firm and freshest buttocks, most profusely wandering, ran.

Some, wantonly, the cleanest hair let down from the ornate nape, and hanging from the occiput, with even discrepancy from a golden binding, dividing here and there over the milky neck. Then beautifully, and with choice polish, sinuated and balled up into an egregious whirl, they elegantly met at the topmost crown, thinned to a point, where a most-precious tuft of pearls, to the highest voluptuousness, bound both the braidings; and beneath, the little breezes masterfully conducted, passed over the flat temples, and arrived, with even thinning, at their seat and voluptuous meeting.

For some the hair was reddish, with much-admired wrappings girdling round the head with an amicable springing floret. Some golden, more than leafy orpiment of outstanding lustre, and of colour, with a decorous whirl composed tendril-wise to the nymphish and voluptuous adornment, with a pretty adjunction of various and precious little stones, and to the expression of extreme delight; some blackish, more than the anthracite plumes of Aesacus, in comely volume shining, covered with limpid golden veils, and above the parting, held with hairpins, with a conspicuous and gracious flight, with manifold curled knots, with much resplendence, with art and artifice that exceeded every wanton study and nymphal voluptuousness — combedly combed, and most-decorously composed. Little snares baited with the retaining, biting, and noxious birdlime of amorous hearts. With whorls of inestimable value hung at the pierced ears, and with most-precious necklaces and segments torqued and girt the straight and snowy throat, with wicked collars sumptuously begemmed, with more excellent research of adornment, and in decorating so much womanly affectation as could never subtly be excogitated.

Oltra di questo alcune di purpurissimi coturni negli pedusculi, cum episphyrii d’oro asseptatamente calciavano. Gli quali sopra il candido pulpamento dille sure terminavano. Ove uno gentilicio ritramato di policaria latitudine circundava di petre pretiose mirificamente exornato, cum laccioli d’oro et di seta. Alcune a pede nudo gli vermiglianti evandii di seta, cum voluptica vanitate gestavano. Molte calciavano soccoli di coreo aureo suppressamente caelati molti eleganti expressi. Assai le crepidule di rosaceo corio portavano orulate d’oro. Tale exquisitamente di expolito et lunato calciamine calciate, overamente cum recurvata apertura, et di amentate solee, cum più nove et maravegliose ligature, ligule et coregie, che mai dir se potesse, di seta caesia et di filamine d’oro, cum gli più vagi et grati implicamenti circa il polposo talo innodantise, che divisare nella mente se valesse, et dall’angusta solea il laqueolo in nodulo bellulamente intricato, di armenica textura usciva impedito tenendo il police digito. Et d’indi verso il minimo demigrava più bianchi che lla calcinatura ossea. Poscia supra le tuberule suffragine scandendo bellissimamente se coniugavano, cum una lingula demissa dal circundante ligamine dil calcaneo supra il culmo dill’eburneo pede, di vermicularia operatura aurea di lucente gemme decorato. Alcune di panno sericeo de raso cum figure per tale artificio congeste, cum variato coloramine volupticamente calciavano, discreto da quello dil superbo et nympheo induto. Concluse decore cum fibule auree, cum adunco morso et illaqueate. Sopra le exquisite crepidule gli tumiduli pecioli suppressi dagli eleganti strevli ansulati d’oro, et gli obstraguli margaritati, et cum nextruli di colorita seta et d’oro cum gli capi di terso argento infixi, erano decentemente innexi ritenuti, cum sì bello decoramento da cecare et exoculare gli effrenati tori. O quanta politura, bellitudine, et nitella, quanto perspicace ornamento, quale insuete operature. Cum quanta acre diligentia vedevase excogitatamente reperto artificio di dare ad gli intuenti dolci, et incentivo piacere, et morire opiculo. Nel vestire similmente concinate oltra la superba et delitiosa inventione tanto decore, quanto condignamente il foelice loco expostulava, cum artificiose fimbrie et lancinule, overo fracte cum subtile intercisura di versicolore et cum mirabile et vermiculario suticio decorissime. Et dal stricto cincto in giù dinanti ordinatamente propendevano gli aurei porpici di vermiculario lavorio. Alcune in loco degli piruli havevano margarite elenchi di crassitudine avellanea. Nel imo cum longitudine fastigiata di alabastrea figura, et similmente nel loco degli porpici aurei, che era una textile lista, overo fasceola aurea di latitudine sextante, di operamento vermiculario. Beyond this, some, with most-purple buskins on the little feet, with gold ankle-pieces, shod themselves trimly. Which terminated upon the white pulp of the calves, where a genteel re-woven band of a policaria's breadth went round, wondrously adorned with precious stones, with little laces of gold and silk. Some, barefoot, bore the vermilion silk sandals with voluptuous vanity. Many were shod with soccoli of golden leather, closely chased with many elegant figures. Very many wore crepidae of rosy leather, edged with gold. Some exquisitely shod with polished, crescent-shaped footwear, or with a recurved opening, and of thonged soles, with more new and marvellous ligatures, laces and thongs than could ever be told, of blue-grey silk and of golden filament, with the vaguest and most-pleasing interlacings knotting round the fleshy heel — more than one could devise in the mind — and from the narrow sole the little snare, prettily entangled in a knot, of Armenian texture, came out, impeded, holding the great toe. And thence toward the least (toe) it passed off, whiter than bone-lime. Then, climbing over the little swelling insteps, most-beautifully they conjoined, with a little tongue let down from the surrounding binding of the heel, over the top of the ivory foot, adorned with wormwork of gold and of lucent gems.

Some, of silken satin cloth, with figures heaped by such artifice, with varied colouring voluptuously shod, distinct from that of the superb and nymphish garb. Concluded comely with gold buckles, with hooked clasp, and ensnared. Over the exquisite crepidae, the little swelling insteps pressed by the elegant little gold-eyeleted straps, and the pearled instep-covers, and with little knots of coloured silk and of gold with the ends of burnished silver fixed, were decently interwoven, retained, with so beautiful an adornment as to blind and put out the eyes of the unbridled bulls. Oh, what polish, beauty and brightness; what perspicacious ornament; what unwonted works! With what keen diligence was seen the thoughtfully-found artifice of giving to the beholders sweet and incentive pleasure, and a little death.

In the dressing, likewise, they were arrayed, beyond the superb and delightful invention, as comely as the happy place condignly demanded, with artful fringes and slashes, or breaks, with subtle intercutting of versicolour, and most-decorously with wondrous wormwork stitching. And from the tight girdle downward, in front, hung in order the golden little tags of wormwork labour. Some, in place of the little pears (tags), had chains of pearls of hazelnut thickness. At the bottom, with a fastigiate length of alabaster figure; and likewise in place of the golden tags, was a woven strip, or golden band, of a sextant's breadth, of wormwork. From its sinuous bosom to the encompassing fringe, coupling among the minute —

Et tale quale le pirulate margarite havevano gemme turbinate cum le complanate inquadrature, et cum le terete cum iucundissimo commercio et elegante consenso dil suo colore et deformatura variatamente dispensate, et summa cum venusta congruentia. Alcune etiam mirai cum superbo et divo culto, cum insigne et egregio, et sumptuosissimo et incredibile exornato, et di pretiositate incomparabile. Le quale sopra gli fianchi indute erano di torace sinuate sericee di amethystino raso luculeo. Et d’indi retro al ultimo spondilo, et al foemine, cum moderata curvescentia dependente, di tomentata foliatura antiquaria, et di mediocre perle contecte. Gli vertigini dilla quale cum nodulature supra le mamillule terminando, et sopra l’ombilico diffinivano in albente. Negli quali erano di pretiosissimi lapilli rosule, et altri mirabili expressi, in oro inclaustrati, et cum inchaustico operamento eximie deornati, et vermiculariamente depoliti. Gli labri dille quale thorace di miro efferato di syrmata textura aurea, gli phrygii iugimente ambivano, et nello infernate extremo alternatamente crassissime perle, et piruli cum moto inconstante ludibonde dependevano. D’indi poscia una investitura di seta verde cum tramatura aurea descendeva, et ad gli genui fina derivava. Di sotto questa fina ad gli tali ultra, un’altra era demissa, di Chermeo intramato d’oro. Le quale cum frequentario pliculamento erano inphimbriate, di latitudine semi et unciale, cum binato ordine negli extremi di crasse margarite. Et nel contento dil phrygio dilla prima inextimabili rhomboidi di gemme occupavano. Tra le quale cum partiario coloramento le circulare interposite lucevano et in campo aureo artificiosamente deputate. Ultra gli labri extremi, erano meruli serratamente formati, et in omni angulo degli quali uno ritondato lapillo pendeva. Et nel imo tra gli meruli instabilmente straleti d’oro ventilabondi. Dal quale phrygio fili d’oro in forma rhombea innodulati descendevano, et da una pillulata gemma retenti. In ciascuna dunque dille sectione interposita era una gemma, per la quale transversariamente uno syrmato filo aureo traiectava. Sopra la prope dicta gemma, nel limbo dil phrygio appacta assideva una circulare simile alle collaterale di forma et crassitudine, cum auree barbule pendente, nel narrato filo transversario, nel mediostimo dilla figura rhombea, uno mirabile lapillo ovolato era intromisso. Et nel mediano dille pleure, similmente infilata era una pillulata gemma, ciascuna cum recta distributione et elegante ordine, et dil coloramento variate aequalmente obviantise. Tutti gli infernati anguli dilla narrata figura intercalariamente, in uno praependeva una inextimabile tabellula quadrata pretiosa, sotto la quale et dalla quale suspeso pendeva uno baccato lapillo. — slashes distributed. And such as the little pear-shaped pearls had turbinate gems with the flattened squarings, and with the rounded ones, with most-pleasant commerce and elegant consent of their colour and fashioning, variously dispensed, and with the utmost comely congruity. Some, too, I beheld with a superb and divine cultivation, with notable and egregious and most-sumptuous and incredible adornment, and of incomparable preciousness. Which, over the flanks, were clad in sinuate silken cuirasses of amethystine bright satin; and thence, behind, to the last vertebra and to the thigh, hanging with moderate curving, of padded antique foliage, and covered with middling pearls. The whorls of which, with little knots ending above the little breasts, and above the navel, defined themselves in white. In which were little roses of most-precious stones, and other marvellous devices, encloistered in gold, and choicely adorned with encaustic work, and wormwork-polished. The lips of which cuirasses, of a wondrously wild train of golden texture, the embroidered bands continuously went round; and at the lower extreme, alternately, thickest pearls and little pears, with inconstant motion, hung playfully.

Thence then a green silken investiture, with golden weft, descended, and reached down to the knees. Beneath this, down past the ankles, another was let fall, of crimson wefted with gold. Which, with frequent little pleating, were fringed, of a half-inch's breadth, with a doubled order at the extremes of thick pearls. And in the field of the first embroidered band, inestimable rhomboids of gems occupied. Among which, with distributive colouring, the circular ones interposed shone, artfully assigned in a golden field. Beyond the outermost lips were merlons serratedly formed, and in every angle of which a rounded little stone hung. And at the bottom, among the merlons, unstably, gold darts fluttering. From which embroidered band gold threads knotted in rhombic form descended, and were retained by a pellet-gem. In each, therefore, of the interposed sections was a gem, through which crosswise a trained gold thread passed. Above the just-said gem, in the border of the band, set fast, sat a circular one like the collateral in form and thickness, with little gold barbs pendent; on the said crosswise thread, in the very middle of the rhombic figure, a marvellous egg-shaped little stone was let in. And in the middle of the sides, likewise threaded, was a pellet-gem, each with straight distribution and elegant order, and of the colouring varied, evenly meeting. All the lower angles of the said figure, intercalated, in one there hung an inestimable square precious little tablet, under which, and from which, suspended, hung a berried little stone. l’altra assula gemmale appensa era una oblonga overo ovola, tutte di mira crassitudine. Il phrygio dilla secunda tra le margarite in forma hemiale erano tabellule di pollicaria longitudine et di semi la latitudine, intervallate tra due circulare, et daposcia tra due quadre, cum altri additamenti di lapilli, negli vacui venustamente appacti et di ornaculi ad delitia et gratia optimamente inserti, da obstupire essa natura, cum iugi fulgetri praelucentissimi fulguranti, et di convenientia dil coloramine iucundissimi, cum le maniche bellissime, et superbi brachiali cum ornato thoracale, et allo exito brachiale et al collare di tali recensiti phrygii, et ornati circundavano bellissime.

Gli brachii bipartitamente erano intecti dallo exito fina al cubito, et dal cubito fina alla comprehensa dilla mano era el manicheto di tuta aurea textura, cum eximie operature sumisse contexto circumvallato d’uno phrygiolo di nympheale studio diligentemente invento nella divisione cubitale bellule illaqueati, cum cordilli aurei, overo cum intortuli funiculi. Nelle divisione era uno elegante exito di copioso grumulo cum curvamine compliculato dilla bombicina nivea tenuissima et nitente interula, nella parte decente invinculati, cum sericei laqueoli geminati in armilete, overo in orbiculi aurei complanati intromessi, et di puro oro mucronati, cum noduli pendenti cum exquisito virginale. Perché quivi il desio, et cupito cum il sapere et potere conciliavase cum la voluntate. Tanto me Iupiter ad gli amorosi ochii quam iucundissimo, che altro veramente non se desiderarebbe, ch’a perpetuo potere mirare, et sì venuste, et di formositate et di delitia, Nymphe praestante cum gli aspecti elicopidi tanto laute, et lascivamente decore, et tale cum nivee veste cum summa politione praenitente.

— the other appended gemmed board was an oblong, or egg-shape, all of wondrous thickness. The embroidered band of the second (nymph), between the pearls in half-round form, were little tablets of a thumb's length and half that breadth, intervalled between two circular ones, and then between two square ones, with other additions of little stones, comelily set fast in the voids, and of little ornaments to delight and grace most excellently inserted — to stupefy nature herself — with continual most-lucent flashings coruscating, and most-pleasant in the convenience of their colouring; with most-beautiful sleeves, and superb arm-pieces with an adorned breast-work; and at the brachial outlet and at the collar, of such recounted embroidered bands, and ornaments, most-beautifully they went round.

The arms were bipartitely covered, from the outlet to the elbow; and from the elbow to the grasp of the hand was the little sleeve, of all-golden texture, woven with choice low-relief work, walled round by a little embroidered band of nymphal study diligently invented, prettily laced at the elbow-division, with gold cords, or with little twisted cordlets. In the divisions was an elegant outlet of a copious little cluster, with a curved little folding of the snowy cotton chemise, most-tenuous and shining, bound in the decent part, with silken little snares doubled into armlets, or let into flattened gold circlets, and pointed with pure gold, with pendent little knots, with exquisite virginal (art). For there desire, and the thing coveted, with the knowing and the power, reconciled itself with the will. So much, by Jove, to the amorous eyes was it most-pleasant, that one would truly desire nothing else but to be able perpetually to gaze — and Nymphs so comely, and of such shapeliness and delight, outstanding, with their bright-eyed aspects so sumptuous, and wantonly comely, and so, with snowy garments of the utmost polish, pre-shining.

Cum tanta provocatione, che all’aspra morte lo homo ultroneamente se offerisca et volente. O contagioso artificio. O allectivo ministerio, o propugnante machine. Qualunque core sano tutissimo, valido et quieto, et libero, et renitente, di facilmente inficere, repente di subvertire et depopulare, sencia mora prosternere, sencia indusio perturbare, totalmente subiugare, et sencia relucto trahere. Di prostrare discussamente omni moderata, incredibile et pervicace continentia. Questi sono quegli veraci et infallibili sateliti dil operoso amore cum ampliabile numero di immanissimi carnifici, le immune remote, et disiuncte pensiculatione insidianti, et da tale inclinante subiectione exclusi di potere per alcuno modo, né cum audente animo prevalerse, et dalle praesentate inevitabile, et trahente dulcitudine, cum tanto solerte et diligente studio illaqueabile, et cum subtile ingegnio invento tanto exquisitissimo operamento et ad tanto bella et formosissima opera per sé dalla foecunda natura, artificio da distrugere ritrovato, et eximio adiuncto et accessorio di cruciare. Omè il quale ragionevolmente fingere se doverebbe, et usare per le marmoree statue, et non per gli humani et fragili cori. Imperoché ancora quel nobile et divo sexo, spoliato et denudato summamente contorque et infice periclitabondi, non che cum questo voluptico additamento di laquei pernitioso investigato. Ma solo questo accede, che elle non pensiculano essere assai il d’amore naturalmente perire, sencia tanto aucto incremento di excogitati modi adiungere gli miseri et molliculi amanti allo interito dil cordolio più facilmente di conducere, et continuamente excitare scintillule extremamente incendiose da vegetare alla consumptione le amorose et fervide flammule, divi obiecti di rimpire et confundere il caldo pecto di bullienti suspiruli, et di fermentare il core ad amore. Hora io non saperei debitamente exprimere per quale modo uno tanto fundamine iacto d’amore stabile et sincero fermamente in Polia collocato, che alquanto quassare il sentisse da queste inevitabile et parate insidie et impetuose violentie. Omè poscia tra me tacitamente replicava. O prophilea Pollia bellatula mia, custodi la tua adepta praeda. Imperoché grande periclitamento, è il transito per tante pyratice fallacie, et manifesti sicarii et praedonuli insidiarii. Gli quali contra omni sincera rectitudine sono dil suo amoroso offendimine commendati et laudabilmente approbati et dagli tristi offensi, tanto più desiderati et riverentemente più requisiti et più dolce amati. Hora nella sua laetissima fronte, sotto a due hemicycle, quale filo di seta subtile, et nigerrime ciglie più che illustrante electro, dui sagittabondi ochii festevolmente lucevano più che irradiantissime stelle nel lympidissimo coelo splendescente. Et nel aspecto suo più belle che incarnate, et ricente rose, et le gene non miltate, ma cum più gratissima et genuina rubedine, che degli

With such provocation that to bitter death a man would offer himself, of his own accord and willing. O contagious artifice! O alluring ministry, O assaulting siege-engines — which any heart, however sound, most safe, valid and quiet, and free, and resisting, you easily infect, suddenly subvert and depopulate, without delay prostrate, without respite perturb, totally subjugate, and without struggle drag away. To lay low, scatteringly, all moderate, incredible and stubborn continence. These are those true and infallible satellites of laborious Love, with an amplifiable number of most-monstrous executioners, ambushing the immune, remote and disjoined reflections, and excluding those inclined to such subjection from being able by any means, nor with a daring spirit, to prevail — and, by the presented, inevitable and drawing sweetness, with so shrewd and diligent study ensnaring, and with subtle wit invented so most-exquisite a working, and to so beautiful and most-shapely a work by fecund nature herself, an artifice found for destroying, and a choice adjunct and accessory of torment. Alas — which reasonably ought to be feigned, and used, for marble statues, and not for human and fragile hearts. For even that noble and divine sex, despoiled and denuded, supremely torments and infects the imperilled, let alone with this voluptuous addition of pernicious snares devised. But this alone accrues: that they do not consider it enough to perish of love naturally, without adding so much increased increment of excogitated modes, to conduct the wretched and soft lovers to the death of heart-grief more easily, and continually to excite little sparks extremely incendiary, to quicken the amorous and fervid little flames to consumption — divine objects, to refill and confound the warm breast with bubbling little sighs, and to ferment the heart to love. Now I would not know how duly to express by what means so great a foundation of love, stable and sincere, firmly placed in Polia, felt itself somewhat shaken by these inevitable and ready ambushes and impetuous violences. Alas, then, within myself I silently repeated: O my dear little beautiful Polia, guard your won prey! For a great peril is the passage through so many piratical fallacies, and manifest cut-throats, and little ambushing plunderers — which, against all sincere rectitude, are for their amorous offence commended and laudably approved, and by the sad, offended ones so much the more desired, and reverently the more sought, and the more sweetly loved.

Now, on her most-joyous forehead, under two half-circles like threads of subtle silk, and blackest brows more than shining amber, two arrow-shooting eyes festively shone, more than the most- irradiating stars resplendent in the most-limpid sky. And in her aspect more beautiful than incarnate and fresh roses; and the cheeks not rouged, but with a most-pleasing and genuine redness, than which —

— apples of Decius (?) in the wine-bearing autumn, reddening, and more lucid than the whitest ivory rubbed smooth, where the Sun showed itself more clear, perhaps, than such a Titan appeared to the Achaemenians (Persians), or to the Egyptians Osiris, or Isis, or Serapis with the calathus, or the three-headed simulacrum, or Mithra in the sacred cave to the Persians — such did the delightful and divine Nymphs expose themselves, at the advent of their lord: conspicuous with venerable semblances, mannered and well-mannered with adorned gestures, most-admired with unvisited beauty, and choice with outstanding gracefulness, illustrious and comely with elegant honesty, and adorned and most-comely with entire boldness.

At this point, deservedly, with a venerable band flying, and with frequent retinue following, the most-beautiful Psyche, full of gratitude, revering her dearest Husband, all easy and blandishing received him; and with the utmost veneration set on his head a most-precious little crown, such as was not the votive one of Hiero. And two of the Nymphs, honourably accompanying her — Imeria the one — placidly received Polia; and the other, Erototimoris, most facetious, took me by the hand. Then, distinctly following, many others, most joyous, three and three, with solemn procession, and order, and most-honorific pomp, and decent veneration, and with nymphal genuflection, came in motion, revering.

And first came Toxodora (Bow-giver), who cunningly offered him the sinuous and wound-dealing bow, which was rigorously extended. She was in the middle of two others: Ennia the one, who in her little swelling hands bore a chiselled little amphora-vase of most-coloured sapphire, with most- pleasant resplendence, with a broadish orifice mosaic-wise carved. They bit the gullet-moulded rim elegantly. — little channels obliquing, excavated with moderate rivulets in-channelled, ending upon a little knot, with a temperate swelling to the shrewd device, with a subjected little foot, stuffed with most-beautiful and manifold flowers, disseminating them. And her consort Phileda (Love-of- pleasure) gathered up in her lap those which Ennia, solacingly scattering, disseminated.

Likewise, between two other most-adorned little Nymphs, came forward Velode; she festively presented to her lord a marvellous quiver, superb of artifice and of egregious invention, with two most-swift arrows — one with the shaft of pure silver, and the other with the pointed shaft of black, heavy, cold and inauspicious lead. That with this he might gird his tender and divine flank, or his shoulders, with the flying weapons. And her companions Omonia (Concord) and Diapraxe (Accomplishment), with two balls, joyfully played, one to the other alternating. But of shining gold was Omonia's, and of fragile crystal her Diapraxe had; and when one of them caught up the gold one, she sent back the crystalline, with a shrewd avoidance of their meeting together.

To these, then, in order, the others followed: the most-beautiful and reverent Typhlote (Blindness), with religious and deserved honours and dignification — she offered, bowing, a most- tenuous veil, that with it he should band and cover his unveiled eyes. She had with her two wanton damsels, of immodest semblance. Of which one, named Asynecha (Heedlessness), turning now to the right side, now to the left, inconstant; and Ardelia Comazista (Busy-Reveller) danced. And like Thymele the histrionic, leaping, a jester, and a notable lamia.

And the other, Aschemosyne (Shamelessness), among all the clothed ones naked, undaunted and salacious, presented herself — neither more nor less than if she had drunk from the fount of Salmacis. And in her left hand she held a sphere formed of golden plates, with its centre. And with her right she would not consent to let her long hair, taken up, extend over the fleshy and quivering buttocks, with unashamed petulance, like a wanton huckstress — but indicating a most-obscene Tribade insolence, with an extolling of her inconstant, blue-grey eyes, and with a lustful act, like a bold Gaditanian (Cadiz dancing-girl), gesticulating with too much lasciviousness, ineptly, and more than the most-filthy Hostius had in himself, gazing in his hollow mirrors.

Newly, three other unpremeditating matrons presented themselves: Teleste (Initiatrix) the first, clad in ardent purple, with her loosed and abundant hair, and on the fillet-bound forehead little curls hanging in front — she, elegant, gave to her god a kindled torch. And a companion, Vrachivia the bearer, gave of an emerald —

— little urn, most-excellently expressed, and of antique artifice, and of an audacity insolent for human workmanship. Its orifice had contorted striations, which striae, or rivulets, ending moderately in the middle amplitude of the vase, with most-even distribution somewhat dilated. Then the comely girt-work descended toward the round bottom, the corpulence little by little thinning, clothed with an inestimable making of celery-leaf foliage, standing forth from the solid with egregious separation; and, continued from the lips of the orifice toward the down-slope, twisting themselves into exquisite foliage, two little handles they rendered, with wondrous relief, and with a little foot.

Out of which broadish orifice, flying and crackling, with a most-pleasing pop, sparks leapt out, running lucent through the air; which then, extinguished, fell as ashen sparks.

The other companion, Capnolina (Smoke-girl), bore a little goatish, or earthen, vase, with a narrow orifice, raised somewhat high and thinning at the bottom; and above its dilation, under the handles, it had circularly, with even distance, thirteen most-measured Greek letters diligently impressed:

ΠΑΝΤΑ ΒΑΙΑ ΒΙΟΥ (PANTA BAIA BIOU)
— "All the things of life are little [trifling / short-lived]."

With many other ornaments and obliquing little channels. Which, from the letters toward the orifice, was polished-smooth. Out of which, and through the vent-holes, leapt forth a misty and sweet-smelling smoke, resolving into nothing through the air.

Recepte finalmente le oblate mysteriose et fatale erotenara et convenienti gestamini. Il divino puello ad sedere se pose sopra uno aureo et antiquario carpento, overo vectabulo ivi triumphale praeparato, tutto di lamine d’oro per tutte le fimbrie circumambiente uno phrygio dodrante in latitudine. Nel quale solariamente lucevano inclaustrate ostentuose gemme di granditudine et inopinabile pretio, et di artificio imperceptibile et di invento rarissime, divinamente disposito, et ordinatamente destinato. Cum due vehiculare rote habente la circunferentia d’oro, et gli radii procedenti dal centrico axe, nel quale il polo iaceva, et infixi nel meditullio erano deformati longiusculi in balaustico figmento di petre pretiose varie di coloramento fulgurante. PANTA [BAIA BIOU]

Received finally the offered mysterious and fateful love-tokens and fitting insignia, the divine boy set himself to sit upon a golden and antique car, or triumphal vehicle, there prepared — all of golden plates, an embroidered band a dodrans wide going round all the fringes. In which, sun-like, shone encloistered ostentatious gems of bigness and unthinkable price, and of imperceptible artifice and rarest invention, divinely disposed, and orderly destined. With two vehicular wheels having the circumference of gold, and the spokes proceeding from the central axle, in which the pole lay; and, fixed in the middle, they were fashioned longish, in balustered figment, of precious stones various of fulgurant colouring.

Sitting, then, he — without protraction of time — both taken and captured were we, Polia and I, by the outstanding Nymphs Plexaura and Ganoma, at the imperial nod of the triumphing, dominant little boy; and, both our arms thrown back behind, and our hands bound at the back, like captives Polia and I were ensnared and bound, connected and re-netted with tresses and little wreaths of roses and various flowering. And behind the pompous and divined car of the prize-bearer and greatest triumphator we were drawn, softly and willingly, by Synesia (Understanding), a most-outstanding Nymph.

For which thing I began almost to tremble. But because the Nymphs, with my world-key Polia, laughed facetiously, of a sudden I was reassured. There followed immediately after us the curious Psyche. And behind her were following the girl-damsels who, venerating, had made offering. Which Psyche was bordered, or clad over, with a matronal and most-ambitious mantle, or chlamys, of gold — such as Silophontes did not give to Darius, nor could Numa, first king and inventor, use such-made. Over the right shoulder, foldedly cast, and eyeleted in pressed gold circlets, where among thick carbuncles most-purged and touchstone-tried, and coruscating with splendour, she held encloistered a diamond an inch-and-a-half long lengthwise and flattened, and a thumb in breadth, and of iron-like scintillation, full of wondrous stupor, with inestimable adornment standing forth — such that of so great a price was not the gift given by Gyges to —

Questo divino triumpho et tutte le triumphale Nymphe praecedevano, como avanti è descripto le pastophore, poscia le trophigere, daposcia le faceferarie, di fascicule d’oro infasciate le face, consequente le splendofore cum auree lucerne, tede, et cerei di albicante, et purissima materia, cum chiaro candore luminosi. Alcune cum vasculi aurei cum angustissimo exito, liquore odoratissimo inconstante sopra tutti guttatamente excussi imbrefacendo conspergevano. Altre assai cum caelico ordine che nelle sue longiuscule mane sonanti instrumenti tenivano. Symphonie suave, fistule forabile, Tibie, cum suavi moduli consonavano, et bucine tortile auree, et pretiosissimamente gemmate. Alcune altre cum gli tintilanti Cymbali. Altri cum increpitanti et rauci Tympani. Sospesi dalla sinistra mano,

— Pythian Apollo. In which — not by the noble Pyrgoteles, but rather divinely — was Cupid most- excellently carved, cruelly lancing himself, and incautious Psyche handling him with the arrow, with a lethal puncture; where the loosed arm bore the flying and vehement golden arrow, or her firebrand; and in the other hand, elegantly cast back with gracious folding over the left shoulder, the chlamys reversed and stuffed with a golden and most-tenuous pile, bearing a hyacinthine lamp of antique workmanship and of perspicuous effigy, kindled, scintillating. Which most-sumptuous chlamys was most- opulently fringed with marvellous precious stones, over a most-exquisite and pleated amice of springing silk and wefted with purest gold, girt under the little swelling breast, with divine and delightful make.

Towing, then, this superb vehicle of triumphant Love drew two scaly serpents, skinks, beyond their nature great in this place, for this amorous office and mystery and conveyance valid and vigorous, inflamed with three-forked and quadruped vibrations; with their necks stretched out, becomingly entwining their scaly breasts with marvellous knotwork of thongs and retainers interwoven of gold train, with little swelling bosses standing forth, of various and excellent gold chasing alternated with gems, most-excellently, with irradiating coruscation, buckled in gold buckles and most-choice clasps they passed through; and thickly adorned with thick little stones, at the drawing not swiftly, but with a temperament of triumphal procession.

This divine triumph all the triumphal Nymphs preceded, as before is described: the shrine-bearers, then the trophy-bearers, then the torch-bearers, the torches banded with little bundles of gold; next the light-bearers with golden lamps, brands, and tapers of whitening and purest material, luminous with clear candour. Continuing, followed the scent-bearers (Osmiferae) and Sweet-smellers (Euosmiae), with golden scent-vessels of unseen make, and with golden censers, or incense-caskets, scattering wondrous fragrance beyond that which the happy place diffusedly breathed everywhere. Some with little golden vases, with the narrowest outlet, sprinkling a most-odorous inconstant liquor drop by drop, bedewing all. Very many others, with a heavenly order, held in their longish hands sounding instruments: sweet symphonies, pierceable pipes, tibiae, sounded with sweet measures; and twisted gold horns, most-preciously gemmed. Some together, with sweetest consent, harmonically, in the Lydian mode, singing, with angelic utterance, and a voice rendering no familiar sound, and reiterating the comely songs. Some others with the tinkling Cymbals. Others with rattling and raucous Drums, suspended from the left hand —

et cum funiculi d’oro et di seta contorquati agli carnosi brachii vicino dilla comprensa dilla mano pendicei. Et poscia cum gli tornatili et longiusculi digiti solicitatamente prohibivano, et a tempo cedevano sopra la pertusata tibia monaula saltanti, non tale da Mercurio reperta, la sibilante voce, che tra gli purpurissimi labri riceveva compositamente il sonoro spirito. Cum gratiosa tuberatione pomululando le colorate buccule. Et daposcia cum la dexterula, la tirata et extenta pelle, cum pulsatoruli di bianchissimo ebure percotevano, cum mensurata participatione et emusicata, cum lene et grato susurro et raucitate, cum il tibiale sibilo concordi risonanti. Alcune cum gemine tibie dill’invento dille quale Marsia ceda, et le phrygie similmente. Alcune cum garulose lyre lyrante, et tale cum cithare Citharizante sopra gli Syrmati fili erei, cum gli teneri et delicati digiti, overo cum plectri l’ereo sono converberando excitavano, et ancora cum altri nobilissimi, et maravegliosi, et cum organati instrumenti, cum aurei et crepitaculi sistri arguto tinnito constrepenti. Et cum calybicei trigonii, cum annuli ludibondi al percusso suave et acuti tintinnanti, et altre conformemente cum adunci cornuli, inaudita harmonia diffundevano, et cum crepitante buccine praecinente. Redimite di diademate di gioie di verno coronamine florente, tra gli variati flori bractee auree deornatamente intercalate, praecipuamente di amethystine viole, et di flori cumatili, et di purpurante amarantho, et holochryso et di cyclamino, et cum strophiole di mellilotho, cum viole luteole et candide, mensuratamente composite, et commixtamente inserte, et di altri coronarii flosculi, et cum candicante margarite et altre gemme, accessoriamente le ornate caesarie circundate et innexe, et alcune elegantissime callicridene tanta demulcente harmonia ad gli auditori communicando dispensavano, quanto forsa unque Apolline, immo sencia haesitare, la lyra pulsante alle helicone Muse non rende, né porge. Né tale et tanta suavitate di sono gli tyrreni navicularii sentirono. Né Arione tanto cum sono supra il delphino vectore ad Tenaro fece il traiecto. Le quale sonatrice, non in uno erano, ma in ordinato processo disposite, et divisione processionale al suo requisito, et constituto loco ad magna extollentia et triumphale adoria et victorine laude, in redundante turme. Per le quale dive cose fermamente mi suado inane praestarse omni foeconda et facunda lingua exprimere satagendo particula degli suavissimi soni, degli dulcissimi cantici, degli solemni et iocosi tripudii et feste, dille dive Nymphe et insigne puelle, dille sue singulare et incomparabile bellece. Dillo excellente et illustre et elegantissimo decoramento, da interrumpere et amputare la gratiosa vita, et da dissipare et distrahere omni duro et obstinato core laetante cum vario et triumphale gestamine et cum alacre

— and, with little cords of gold and silk twisted, hanging at the fleshy arms near the grasp of the hand. And then, with their turned and longish fingers, solicitously they forbade, and in time yielded, upon the pierced tibia, leaping — not such as was found by Mercury — the whistling voice which, among the most-purple lips, received composedly the sonorous breath. With gracious swelling, apple-rounding the coloured little cheeks. And then, with the little right hand, the drawn and stretched skin they beat with little beaters of whitest ivory, with measured and mosaic participation, with a soft and grateful murmur and raucousness, resounding in concord with the tibia's whistle.

Some with twin tibiae — to the invention of which let Marsyas yield, and the Phrygians likewise. Some lyring with garrulous lyres, and some citharising with citharas, exciting upon the trained brazen strings, with tender and delicate fingers, or with plectra, the brazen sound, striking it together; and again with other most-noble and marvellous and organed instruments, with golden and rattling sistra clattering with a sharp tinkle; and with steel triangles, with playful rings tinkling, soft and sharp, at the sweet stroke; and others conformably with hooked little horns, diffused an unheard harmony, with a crackling trumpet fore-singing.

Wreathed with diadems of jewels, flowering with a spring-crown, among the varied flowers gold foil becomingly intercalated — chiefly of amethystine violets, and of sky-blue flowers, and of purpling amaranth, and everlasting, and of cyclamen, and with little wreaths of melilot, with yellow and white violets, measuredly composed, and mixedly inserted, and of other coronary florets, and with whitening pearls and other gems, accessorily their ornate locks girdled round and woven in. And some, most-elegant, of beautiful voice, dispensed so much soothing harmony, communicating it to the hearers, as perhaps Apollo — nay, without hesitating — playing the lyre to the Heliconian Muses does not render, nor offer. Nor such and so great a sweetness of sound did the Tyrrhenian sailors hear; nor did Arion, with so much sound, upon the dolphin his conveyer, make the crossing to Taenarum. Which women musicians were not in one place, but disposed in ordered procession, and processional division at their requisite and constituted place, to great extolling and triumphal glory and victorine praise, in redundant troops.

Through which divine things, I firmly persuade myself, every fecund and eloquent tongue would prove vain, striving to express a particle of the sweetest sounds, of the sweetest canticles, of the solemn and playful dances and feasts, of the divine Nymphs and notable girls, of their singular and incomparable beauties. Of the excellent and illustrious and most-elegant adornment — enough to interrupt and amputate gracious life, and to dissipate and distract every hard and obstinate heart — rejoicing with various and triumphal insignia, and with alacrity —

Chapter XXIII

p. 358confidence: mediumverified

Dal quale appresentati fussimo dinanti al sacro sancto fonte cythereo.

POLIPHILO IL MIRABILE ARTIFICIO DIL VENEREO FONTE DESCRIVE NEL CENTRO DILLA THEATRALE AREA EXISTENTE, ET COME FRACTA FUE LA CORTINETTA. ET VIDE LA DIVINA MATRE IN SUA MAIESTATE, ET COME ESSA SILENTIO ALLE CANTANTE NYMPHE IMPOSE. DILLE QUALE TRE PER UNO A POLIA ET A LLUI GLI CONSIGNOE. DAPOSCIA CUPIDINE AMBI DUI GLI FERITE, ET LA DEA CUM L’AQUA DIL FONTE GLI IMBREFECE, ET POLIPHILO FUE REVESTITO. POSTREMO VENENDO MARTE IMPETRATA LA LICENTIA SE PARTIRONO.

CUM DECENTISSIME VENERATIONE ET summa honorificentia la Eutrapela Polia et io affectuosamente dinanti al mysterioso fonte dilla divina genitrice congenulati, io da una imperceptibile dulcedine diffusamente invadere vexabonda me sentiva, che in quel puncto che fare non sapeva. Imperò che per lo amoenissimo et incredibilmente delitioso sito, et ultra omni credito dilectoso di virentia et vernale decoramento. Et per le avicule per il purissimo aere discorrendo, et per le novelle fronde garriendo volante ad gli forinseci sensi, quam iucundissime, et cum le decorissime Nymphe cantante melode inseme cum gli sui insueti soni audiendo, et degli sui divi acti, et modestissime moventie videndo ardentemente impulso ad extrema voluptate, et de sì facta fabricatura di tanta dignitate di concepto, et dilla elegantissima dispositione solertemente et curioso pensiculando, et di tanta inexperta fragrantia avidamente hauriendo. Per lo immortale Iove rectamente ignorava ad quale sensitivo potere, lo intento obiecto mio da tanto diverso dilecto distracto, et excessivo et foelice solatio et voluptico placimine firma, et stabilmente applicare, et nescio me accusava. Le quale tutte belle, et dulcissime cose tanto più gratioso et desiderabile allectamento ad me allhora plenamente causando offerivano, quanto che io conosceva la Uranothia Polia participe placidamente delectarsene. In questo loco, at etiam di questo ammirando fonte la novitate et excellentia mirando.

(the last line of the preceding chapter) …by which we were brought before the holy and sacred Cytherean fountain.

Notes

Poliphilo describes the marvellous artifice of the Venerean fountain standing at the centre of the theatre's floor, and how the little curtain was torn away; and he saw the divine Mother in her majesty, and how she imposed silence upon the singing nymphs, of whom she assigned three apiece to Polia and to him. Afterwards Cupid wounded them both, and the goddess sprinkled them with the water of the fountain, and Poliphilo was clothed again. Last of all, Mars coming, they obtained leave and departed.

With the most fitting reverence and the highest honour, the graceful Polia and I knelt devotedly before the mysterious fountain of the divine mother, and I felt an imperceptible sweetness spreading through me and troubling me, so that in that moment I did not know what to do. For what with the delightful place, incredibly pleasing and beyond all belief lovely in its greenness and spring adornment; and the little birds coursing through the purest air and chattering as they flew among the new leaves, most joyfully, to the outward senses; and the hearing of those most beautiful nymphs singing their melodies together with their unfamiliar instruments; and the sight of their divine gestures and most modest movements, which drove me ardently toward the extremity of pleasure; and the careful, curious weighing of a fabric of such dignity of conception and such elegant arrangement; and the greedy drinking-in of a fragrance I had never known — by immortal Jove, I truly did not know to which of my senses I should firmly and steadily apply the object of my attention, distracted as it was by so many different delights, by so excessive and happy a solace and voluptuous pleasing; and I accused myself of not knowing.

And all these beautiful and most sweet things offered me an enticement so much the more gracious and desirable, in that I knew that Polia — heavenly Polia — was placidly taking her share of the delight in them.

In this place, then, and marvelling too at the novelty and excellence of this astonishing fountain.

---

Confidence: medium. The chapter argument is secure. The body is one enormous periodic sentence — a chain of per… clauses hung on "I did not know to which sense to apply my attention" — and English will not hold a period that long, so I have broken it with semicolons. The sense is not in doubt; the shape has been compromised, and a reviewer might well restore more of it than I dared.

  • The chapter argument is set in full capitals in the original, as the running
  • The final sentence is deliberately unfinished: the argument runs straight on
  • per il purissimo aere discorrendo — discorrere here in its older sense

Il quale nel mediostimo di questo inhumano aedificamento divinamente constructo et expresso per questo modo. Della nigerrima petra, che sola integramente era tutto il silicato, overamente il pavimento di l’area di essa propria nel mediano di uno murulo sublato pedale, cum egregia politura reducta cum omni ornato opportuno di forma extrinseca heptagono, et della interstitia rotunda. Cum l’ambiente cimasula et socco et arulette, et undiculatione fabrefacte et ordinariamente supposite alle base, sopra il mediano puncto degli anguli, dil quale per ciascuno era superastructa una enthesiata, overo ventriculata columna in numero septe, cum summa exquisitura turbinate. Dille quale due correspondevano aequale ex adverso dell’ingresso. In conspecto di questo ove stavamo proni geniculati. Una dille quale tornatile columne, alla dextera parte cyanava praefulgente di finissimo sapphyro, et dalla sinistra vernava virente smaragdo di praestantissimo colore più lucentissimo che gli affixi per gli ochii al Leone al tumulo di Hermia Regulo. Né tale fu donato da Ptolemaeo ad Lucullo. Né di tanta pretiositate fue il praesentato da Re di Babylonia al Re Aegyptico, né di tanto aestimamento furono quegli dil obelisco nel Tempio di Iove. Né di tanta miraveglia fue la statua nel templo di Hercule in Tyro, quale questo admirando se praestava. Proximo ad questa sequiva una columna di petra turchinia di venusto Ceruleo coloratissima, cum la virtute gratiosamente donata. Et quantunque caeca, niente dimeno illustrissima et specularmente praefulgeva.

Contigua alla sapphyrica columna assideva una pretiosa di petra caeca etiam di iucundissimo colore, quale il Meliloto, et di lustro quale lo interlucido floreo dil vatrachio. Adhaeriva a questa una di Iaspide di colore hyalino, et l’altra di topatio fulgurante colore aureo. La septima sola et singularmente era hexagonia di lympidissimo berillo indico di oleaceo nitore in contrario gli obiecti reddendo. Et questa per medio dille due prime correspondeva, per che omni figura dispare angulare, uno angulo obvia nel mediano dell’intercalato di dui. Dunque il circulo obducto del suo diametro semisse, ivi uno triangulo aequilatero constituito, et poscia dal centro una linea, nel medio dilla linea

Which fountain, at the very middle of this more-than-human building, was divinely constructed and set out in this manner.

Of that blackest stone which alone, and entire, made the whole paving — that is, the floor of the area — there rose at its centre a little wall a foot high, brought to an excellent polish and furnished with every fitting ornament, heptagonal on the outside and round within. It had its running cymatium and socle, its small plinths and its wave-mouldings, wrought and set in due order beneath the bases; and over the middle point of each angle there was built up a column swelling in the shaft — that is, bellied — seven of them in all, turned with the utmost exquisiteness. Two of these stood answering one another, equally, opposite the entrance, in full view of where we knelt face down.

One of these lathe-turned columns, on the right hand, shone blue with the finest sapphire; and on the left one glowed spring-green with an emerald of a colour so surpassing, and more brilliant, than the stones set for eyes in the lion on the tomb of Hermias the prince. Nor was such a one given by Ptolemy to Lucullus; nor was that of so great price which the King of Babylon presented to the King of Egypt; nor were those of the obelisk in the Temple of Jove of such estimation; nor was the statue in the temple of Hercules at Tyre so great a marvel, as this one showed itself to be.

Next to this followed a column of turquoise stone, most beautifully coloured a lovely cerulean, endowed graciously with its virtue; and though it was an opaque stone, it was nonetheless most brilliant, and shone back like a mirror.

Touching the sapphire column there stood another precious one, opaque also, of a most delightful colour like melilot, and with a lustre like the translucent flower of the buttercup. Adjoining this was one of jasper of a glassy colour, and the other of topaz flashing with a golden colour. The seventh, alone and singularly, was hexagonal, of the most limpid Indian beryl with an oily sheen, giving back whatever stood before it reversed.

And this one answered to the midpoint between the first two — because every figure of an odd number of angles has one angle facing the middle of the interval between two others. So then: the circle drawn about its half-diameter, an equilateral triangle set up within it, and then a line from the centre, and in the middle of that line —

Confidence: medium. The gem-lore and the column order are secure — Colonna names each stone plainly and the Plinian comparisons are identifiable. Two weaker areas: the mouldings of the kerb (cimasula, socco, arulette, undiculatione) are a technical vocabulary I have rendered by their standard Vitruvian equivalents without being certain Colonna means exactly those members; and the geometrical construction in the last paragraph is compressed to the point of obscurity, so I have translated it literally rather than smoothing it into a proof. A reviewer should look hardest at the last paragraph.

sopra la circunferentia adiacente deducta, tanto è la septenaria divisione dilla dicta circulare figura. In medio del scapo della septima columna beryllia, dalla parte intranea era mirificamente della propria petra quasi di scalptura divulsa, uno puerulo Hermaphrodito da uno cotylidone ritinuto. Le tre praelucente columne all’ordine dextero similmente per ciascuna haveano in miro modo uno infantulo capto in certi acceptabuli. Et cusì nel scapo di ciascuna dille pretiose columne sinistre appendeva per ciascuna infixo il foemello sexo. Et questo de artificio mystico nel medio dille columne era naturalmente expresso. Cum tanto scintulamento di lustratione, quale non rende la corrosione dil cotes, overo smirillo cum la lambente Tripolea creta. Le base, gli capitelli, il trabe Phrygio et coronice extavano di mundissimo oro. Gli archi cum tutto il solido tra una columna et l’altra era della subacta petra di una delle columne per ordine ambiente, cioè di saphyro verso il smaragdo et il smaragdo verso la turchinia. Et cusì subsequentemente tutto l’arcuato era mirabilmente constructo. Negli anguli dilla corona sopra la viva et centrica linea perpendiculare di qualunque substituta columna, una Aruleta, et di supra excitata una imagine di planita cum il suo appropriato attributo promineva. La sua grandecia dal tertio dilla subiecta columna exacta symmetricamente di purissimo oro. Nel fronte anteriore alla dextera il falcifero Saturno assideva, et alla sinistra la noctiluca Cynthia, per ordine incominciando dal primo circinanti terminavano ad Selene. Sotto agli quali nel zophoro in circuito cum maximo exquisito di artificio elegantemente celati vedevase gli duodeci signi zodiaci, cum le superiore impressione, et charactere, cum eximia scalptura expressi. Il culmo poscia di questo mirabilissimo fonte et tectorio fulgeva di una insolente cupula di optimo et disvenato crystallo mundissimo et perspicuo. Né tale vide Xenocrates, né reperto simile in Cypro, né producto in Asia. Né in Germania, sencia rubigine et scabricie, sencia nube maculosa, né centro sale, né alcuno capillamento vedevasi. Né tale franse Nerone. Ma puro praestante et asyntheto, incincta di una sublata operatura cum aequa convenientia di ligatura di fronde procedente da alcuni monstriculi cum pueruli per quelle ludibundi maravegliosamente implexi. La quale era di egregia corpulentia et convexo. Nel gracilamento del summo cacumine havea infixo uno miraculoso ostentamento in uno aureo et faberrimo Lovo, di uno undique fulgitritio carbunculo di forma ovola et di crassitudine strutiocamela. Nelle facie dil murulo dilla fuscatissima petra, sopra il quale emusicatamente

— drawn upon the adjacent circumference: such is the sevenfold division of the said circular figure.

In the middle of the shaft of the seventh, the beryl column, on the inner face, there was marvellously carved out of that same stone — as though wrenched free of the sculpture — a little Hermaphrodite child, held in a socket. The three shining columns of the right-hand order had likewise, each of them, an infant boy caught in a kind of receptacle; and so too on the shaft of each of the precious columns of the left there hung, set into it, the female sex. And this, by a mystical artifice, was expressed naturally at the middle of the columns, with such a scintillation of polish as neither the biting of whetstone nor emery with licking Tripoli chalk can give.

The bases, the capitals, the Phrygian architrave and the cornice stood out in the purest gold. The arches, and all the solid between one column and the next, were of the wrought stone of one of the columns of the surrounding order — that is, of sapphire toward the emerald, and of emerald toward the turquoise — and so on in sequence, the whole arcade was marvellously constructed.

At the angles of the crown, above the true central perpendicular of each column beneath, there stood a small altar, and rising from it projected the image of a planet with its proper attribute, its size exactly a third of the column below it, symmetrically, and of the purest gold. On the front face, at the right, sat scythe-bearing Saturn, and at the left the night-shining Cynthia; and beginning from the first and going round in order they ended at Selene. Beneath these, in the frieze running round, chased with the most exquisite artifice, were to be seen the twelve signs of the zodiac, with their governing impressions and characters, expressed in outstanding carving.

The crown then of this most marvellous fountain, and its roofing, blazed with an extraordinary cupola of the finest crystal, veinless, utterly pure and transparent. Xenocrates saw none such; none like it was found in Cyprus, nor produced in Asia, nor in Germany — without rust or roughness, without a clouded speck, no core of salt, nor any hairline flaw to be seen. Nor did Nero shatter such a one. But pure, surpassing, and unalloyed, it was girdled with a raised work, in a fitting and even binding of foliage issuing from certain little monsters, with small boys wonderfully interwoven playing among them; and it was of excellent bulk and convexity.

At the tapering of its topmost peak was fixed a miraculous show: in a golden and most cunningly wrought [?setting], a carbuncle flashing lightning on every side, egg-shaped, and of the thickness of an ostrich's.

On the faces of the little wall of that darkest stone, upon which —

HOSPER SPINTHER KELETHMOS. Tripedale ciascuna facia. Et dalle base auree fina al trabe l’altitudine extava di pedi septe. L’artificio dilla quale cosa mirando et expolitissimo tacendo più aptamente riservata sarà la dignitate sua reputo dunque che penurioso et ieiunamente disertabondo praestarme. Quivi tra la columna saphyrica et smaragdinea se contineva in orbiculi flexi cum laqueoli innodati una la più bella cortinetta velacea impexa, che unque la foetosa natura ad gli dii cosa più grata di producere excogitato havesse potuto, textile tanto bella et di materia, che io non saperei unque exprimere. Ceda meritamente quivi la mirabile cortina mandata dagli Samii a Delphi. Questa summamente appareva come pretiosissimo thesoro gratiosa alla mia Polia. La quale velando occultava la maiestale et divina praesentia dilla veneranda matre. Diqué essendo ambidui Polia et io supra gli vertibili popliti expositi cernui, il divino signore Cupidine, dede alla Nympha Synesia la sagitta d’oro et accortamente gli fece nuto che ad Polia essa la offerisca. Et che ella cum la dicta metuenda sagitta lacere, et sfinda la nobilissima cortina. Ma Polia di ciò quasi dolentise del iusso di tale scissura et fractura, quantunque subiecta si fusse ad quello imperio divino parea inexperta recusando di non assentire. Il signore in quel medesimo momento surridendo iniunse alla Nympha Synesia, quella la dovesse alla Nympha Philedia consignare. Et ella poscia ad me la praesentasse. Et che quello che la mellea et integerrima Polia fare non audeva, che io thelithoro et avidissimo di mirare la Sanctissima genitrice exequire dovesse. Laonde non cusì praesto il divino instrumento tractai. Che di caeca flamma circumacto non ricusando, immo cum urgente affecto proiectissimo la cortinetta percossi. Et nel sfindirsi, quasi che Polia vidi contristarsene, et la columna smaragdina scloppando parve che tutta si dovesse fragmentare. Et ecco repente che io la divina forma nel salso fonte palesemente vedo exprompta dalla veneranda maiestate, dilla quale omni pulchritudine delitiosamente emanava. Né più presto quel aspecto inexpectato z

— the ready columns were raised, there were cut, perfectly excavated, letters of the most ancient Greek, the shaft being three-quarters of a foot. In the hollows of these there shone, laid in, words of refined silver. On the front face only two letters were to be seen, intersected with companion emblems of gold and elegantly polished; and then following, on the other faces, three and three, they said this:

ΩΣΠΕΡ ΣΠΙΝΘΗΡ ΚΗΛΗΘΜΟΣ

as a spark, so enchantment

Each face was three feet, and from the golden bases up to the architrave the height stood at seven feet. Of the artifice of this thing, marvellous and most highly finished, I judge that its dignity will be more fittingly preserved by my keeping silent than by my discoursing of it poorly and meagrely.

There, between the sapphire and the emerald column, hung on bent rings with knotted loops the most beautiful little veiling curtain that fertile nature could ever have devised to produce as a thing more pleasing to the gods — so beautiful a weaving, and of such a material, that I should never know how to describe it; but of a sandalwood colouring, woven with the loveliest flowering, and with four Greek letters of gold subtly embroidered over it, ΥΜΗΝ, stretched out most becomingly and heavenward. Let the marvellous curtain the Samians sent to Delphi rightly yield place here. This appeared to my Polia as a most precious treasure, and gracious; and it was this that veiled and hid the majestic and divine presence of the venerable mother.

And so, both of us — Polia and I — kneeling face down upon our bending knees, the divine lord Cupid gave the golden arrow to the nymph Synesia, and shrewdly made her a sign that she should offer it to Polia, and that Polia with that fearful arrow should rend and split the most noble curtain. But Polia, as though grieved at the command for such a cutting and breaking, however subject she was to that divine authority, seemed unpractised, and refused to consent. The lord in that same moment, smiling, charged the nymph Synesia that she should hand it to the nymph Philedia, and that she should then present it to me; and that what the honeyed and most upright Polia did not dare to do, I — [?spellbound], and most greedy to look upon the most holy mother — should carry out.

Whereupon no sooner had I taken hold of the divine instrument than, ringed about with a blind flame and not refusing, but rather with an urgent and headlong passion, I struck the little curtain. And as it split I saw that Polia was almost saddened by it; and the emerald column, cracking, seemed as though it must shatter entirely.

And behold, all at once I plainly see the divine form in the salt fountain, brought forth out of that venerable majesty from which all beauty deliciously flowed. Nor sooner had that unlooked-for sight —

Notes

Confidence: medium. The narrative — Polia's refusal, the arrow passing from Synesia to Philedia to Poliphilo, the tearing, the cracking column, the revelation — is secure and is the point of the chapter. Three softer places: the inscription's letter-count does not close (below), thelithoro is a coinage I cannot place and is marked, and salso fonte is either a real detail or a slip. A reviewer should start with the inscription and with thelithoro.
