divino ad gli ochi mei spirando scorse, che ambi dui da extrema dolcecia excitati, et da novello et da tanto diutinamente concupito piacere impulsi et velitati, cum divoto timore inseme quasi in extasi rimansimo. Per la quale cosa cusì ritrovantime incomminciai pur ragionevolmente expaventarme alquanto dubitando dilla visione in la valle Gargaphya che hebbe il filio di Aristeo, in uno momento maraveglia et terriculo mi misse. In medio la divina Venere stavasi nuda nelle perspicue, et limpidissime aquule insino supra ad gli ampli et divi fianchi. Le quale non crasso, non gemino, non disfracto, non breve il cythereo corpo reddendo, ma integerrimo et simplice, quale era cusì in ipso perfectamente se cerniva. Et circumcirca all’infimo grado suboliva uno spumamento che referiva olido musco. Cum tanto numine trasparente il divino corpo, cum praecipua perspicuitate quella maiestate et venerabile aspecto obiectantise. Quanto pretioso et corruscante carbunculo agli solarii radii fulgura. Cum facteze et mirando composito tra gli humani né viduto né unque meditato. La quale havea, o quanta cum venustate la sua obaurea caesarie amoena et delicatamente compta, supra la lactea et candifica fronte concinnamente irriciatula et concrispulata cum erranti, et inquietuli vertigini, che di extendirsi erano capreolamente impediti. Et dalle rosee spalle, da bellissime undicule alla sua libera effusione decoramente prohibiti. La facia rosea nivante. Gli ochii syderei et luminosi cum amoroso, et sanctissimo obtuto.Le melule gene purpuree. La bucca angustula et purpurissimamente coralicea. Domicilio et praediolo di qualunque fragrante germine. Il pecto più che niveo thesaurizato, cum due tuberule mamillule omni inclinatione reluctante. Il corpo eburissimamente glabello. Divini sembianti. Ambrosio immo di moscamine spirante spirito. Il capillamento decorissimo poscia quale tenuissimi fili aurei syrmati, supra le purgatissime aque, non summergibili, ma in gyro sparsi longissimi supernatabuli. Nel ostento aemuli dil comoso Phoebo nel sudo Olympo, gli illuminanti radii irradiante. Et supra li torcularei crinuli parte dilla venustissima fronte, cum densa sobole et spiroso cumulamine praenitendo anteventuli et umbriculariamente contegenti fina alle exigule aure. Dalle quale pendevano due ostentose margarite, quale ad essa nel Pantheon il dissecto Unione a Roma non pendeva. Né mai tale produsse la Taprobane insula di candore conspicue, ambiva una circinatura, overo strophia implectante di vermiglie albicante et amoene rose verneamente intexta cum gemmule fulgurava.

— that divine sight breathed upon my eyes and passed into them, but both of us, roused by an extreme sweetness and driven and harried by a new pleasure so long desired, remained together in a devout fear, almost in ecstasy.

And finding myself so, I began with some reason to be afraid, misdoubting the vision that the son of Aristaeus had in the vale of Gargaphie; and in a moment it put both wonder and terror into me.

In the midst, the divine Venus stood naked in the clear and most limpid little waters, up to her ample and divine flanks — waters which gave back the Cytherean body neither thickened nor doubled nor broken nor foreshortened, but wholly and simply, so that it was perfectly discerned in them just as it was. And round about, at the lowest level, there rose a foaming that gave off a fragrance of musk. With such divinity was that body transparent, with such particular clarity did that majesty and venerable aspect present itself, as a precious and coruscating carbuncle flashes in the rays of the sun — with features and a marvellous composition neither seen nor ever imagined among human kind.

She had — oh, with what loveliness — her golden hair pleasantly and delicately dressed, neatly curled and crisped above the milky whitening brow with wandering and restless whorls, which were hindered from spreading out like vine-tendrils; and from her rosy shoulders they were becomingly kept from falling free by the most beautiful little waves. The face rosy and snowing. The eyes starry and luminous, with an amorous and most holy regard. The honeyed cheeks purpled. The small narrow mouth most purple, of coral — the dwelling and the little estate of every fragrant bud. The breast more than snowy and treasured up, with two small swelling paps that resisted every inclination. The body most ivory and smooth. Divine semblances. Ambrosial — breathing, rather, a spirit of musk.

Her hair then, most beautiful, like the finest golden threads trailing, lay upon the purest waters, not sinking, but scattered in a gyre and floating out very long: in that show the rivals of long-haired Phoebus in the clear Olympus, radiating illuminating rays. And above the twisted little locks, over part of the loveliest brow, with a dense growth and an airy heaping, they shone out before her and covered her as with a little shade, down to the small ears. From these hung two ostentatious pearls, such as the divided Union did not hang for her in the Pantheon at Rome; nor did the island of Taprobane ever produce their like for conspicuous whiteness. And a circlet went about her, or rather a plaited band of vermilion-whitening and pleasant roses, springlike interwoven, and it flashed with little gems.

Notes

Confidence: medium. This is a blazon — a head-to-toe catalogue — and the sense of every clause is clear; the difficulty is that Colonna coins a diminutive or a compound in nearly every one (melule gene, eburissimamente glabello, torcularei crinuli), and English has to choose between keeping the strangeness and keeping the sentence readable. I have kept the strangeness where it carries meaning and let it go where it was only ornament, which is a judgement another translator could reasonably make differently. Nothing here is doubtful; a good deal is debatable.

Et nel ambito internate del sacratissimo fonte, fora dille haesione de gli sumptuosi gradi. Il floribundo et purpureo Adone germinava tra le sue amnice fronde purpurigiante dall’aqua exclusivo. Et al lato sinistro similmente cum le sue pallide uve thelygono floriva. Et alla dextera Arsenogono, spectatissime herbe et sempre floribonde. Et in circo alla dea, alcune candide columbule volitavano, moratamente obsequibile ministrante. Cum gli aurei rostri nelle mundissime limphe immersi. Il cythereo corpusculo mysteriosamente rorefacevano. Le guttule dunque altramente non apparendo supra la traslucida carne, che perle orientale affixe. D’indi Peristeria Nympha ad gli venerei famulitii et ministrato sedulo ad lei stante, cum intento animo procacemente paratissima. Esse gratiose filie et ancillule, cum prompto effecto della dea matre. Postremamente retiniva, daposcia nelle divine mano una aperta ostrea, stipata di fresche et vernee rose, et nell’altra una facula ardente. Hora dal supremo grado, supra il quale le columne extavano, fina al limito dil fonte sei graduli ancora descendevano di fusco achate, et cusì il piano fondo inundulato dil più bello et gratioso, et vago varicamine lacteo strumantise, et variamente incocleantise, che unque ad gli sensi iucundo obiecto opponere si potesse. L’aqua fontanicia fina al limbo dil quarto grado attingeva, gli altri dall’aqua immuni. Modo supra il superiore grado uno lascivo in specie homo, et Divo Nyctileo ociosamente sedeva. L’aspecto dil quale, di una petulante, et insigne fanciulla se obiectava facetissima. Cum il pecto per il discrimine detecto. And within the inner circuit of the most sacred fountain, clear of the join of the sumptuous steps, flowering, purple Adonis sprang up among its kindred leaves, purpling, kept just clear of the water; and on the left side likewise thelygonon flowered with its pale grape-clusters, and on the right arsenogonon — most admirable herbs, and always in flower. And in a circle about the goddess certain small white doves fluttered, dutifully and decorously ministering: dipping their golden bills in the most spotless waters, they mysteriously bedewed the little Cytherean body, and the droplets showed on her translucent flesh as nothing other than orient pearls set there. Close by stood the nymph Peristeria, promptly and even boldly ready, attending with a whole mind upon the services of Venus.

Likewise outside the fountain, upon the stone floor where Peristeria stood, at the right side, three other divine girls stood naked in this manner, inseparably embraced: two of them, Eurydomene and Eurymone, showing their maiden faces toward us; the third, Eurymeduse, turned with her whitest shoulders to us, her hidden hips veiled by the long outpouring of her most golden hair. These were the gracious daughters and handmaids, prompt to the effect of the goddess mother. And she held, lastly, in her divine hands an opened oyster-shell packed with fresh spring roses, and in the other a burning torch.

Now from the topmost step, upon which the columns stood, six little steps more went down to the lip of the fountain, of dark agate; and the flat floor beneath the water was wave-worked with the loveliest and most graceful milky veining, variously spiralling — as delightful an object as could ever be set before the senses. The fountain's water reached to the edge of the fourth step; the others stood clear of it.

And above the upper step there sat at his ease a wanton figure in the shape of a man, the god Nyctelius, whose look was that of a pert and striking girl, most witty, the breast bared by the parting of his garment. His horned head was bound with a vine-band of writhen tendrils, adorned with savoury grape- clusters. He leaned upon two most swift tigers.

And on the left in the same manner sat, at her ease, a most beautiful and nourishing matron, her broad and curled brow wreathed with a blond garland of wheat-ears. That renowned lady rested upon two scaled serpents. And each of the two held a spherical ball of some thin and yielding stuff in their laps; and with these, at measured intervals, through a cunning and fateful nippled opening, they dripped into the fountain, drop by drop, a most sweet, foaming and potent liquor. Per questa divina dispositione dunque la sanctissima maiestate dilla Dea resideva volupticamente nel mediastimo del fonte. Et la parte che del divino corpo tra l’aque stasea, cusì né più, né meno, che radio overo splendore del Sole in expolitissimo crystallo praelucente. Quivi congenulati divotamente perseverando, cum la mente oltra modo, et excessivamente mirato vacillava. Non valendo fixamente el nume divino dovunque spirante mirare. Et la cagione non meno pensiculatamente recogitando, cum quale dolcecia di sorte et fede, cum quale modo et merito, tali mysterii ad me fusse concesso chiaramente di cernere, et cum gli ochii mei ad tale obiecto indispositi. Ma solamente iudicai essere stata degli immortali Dii la libera voluntate, et il benigno consentire di Polia et fidele oratione. Ma supra tutte cose ad me praestavasi displicibile, che tra tante coeleste et dive persone solo contemptibile et exotico, et de gli decoti habiti atriti et frustrati, et di qualunque altra maniera dissimile istava abiectissimo et pauperrimo, et allhora mi sarebbe stato il modo di coprire la mia deformitate quam acceptissimo. Quale Erichthonio per caelare gli viperini pedi. Imperò di incredibile miraveglia stupefacto nell’animo mio summamente la benignitate divina laudava. Che permesso haveva, che terrigeno homo le opere divine et il thesoro dilla fermentosa natura palesemente contemplasse. Per la quale cosa, quelle insigne Nymphe che di sotto le pergule, cum gli sui plausi et cantici et suave harmonia laetabonde festizavano per la victa praeda, che triumphante consequire doveva il pinnato et temerario Cupidine che più perspicace dil Lynceo et di Argo oculeo, era vigorosamente cum l’arme prompto. Diqué parv’hora consumpta dalla dea Matre, ad gli coelesti soni, et cantici alquanto de silentio intervallato ad ambi nui, cum divina facundia et lepore mulcente cum blando affamine tale suaviloquio la sanctissima bucca produsse parole argute et sencia dubio, da soporare et la vigilante custodia adormentare dil fatale thesoro di Colcho. Et da rivocare in benigno effigiato Aglauros filia di Cecrope. Et al grato armento di ristituere Daphni Idaeo, cum la forma humana. Et Cadmo et Hermione dalla sibilante voce rivocare, et dal squammeo corpo, ad Polia cusì dicendo. Pulchella Polia cultrice mia, gli tui sancti libamenti saeduli obsequii,

— they took care to dip their little rosy feet in the salt fountain; and of their ready little toes one stood out beyond the rest, the one nearest the great toe. Then the others, step by step, with graceful measure, sloped down toward the rounded ankle, diminishing in good order toward the outer edge.

By this divine arrangement, then, the most holy majesty of the goddess sat voluptuously in the very middle of the fountain; and the part of the divine body that stood among the waters showed neither more nor less than a ray or splendour of the sun shining through the most polished crystal.

Kneeling there and devoutly persevering, my mind wavered, gazing beyond all measure and to excess, unable to look fixedly upon the divine spirit breathing everywhere. And no less thoughtfully I turned over the cause — by what sweetness of fortune and faith, by what means and merit, such mysteries were granted me to see clearly, and with my eyes unfit for such an object. But I judged it to be only the free will of the immortal gods, and the kind consent and faithful prayer of Polia. Above all things it displeased me that among so many celestial and divine persons I alone stood there contemptible and foreign, in worn and threadbare clothes, unlike them in every way, most abject and most poor; and I would then most gladly have had some means of covering my deformity — as Erichthonius had, to hide his viper feet. And so, stupefied in my mind with incredible wonder, I praised the divine benignity to the utmost, that it had allowed an earth-born man openly to contemplate the works of the gods and the treasure of fermenting nature.

Whereupon those illustrious nymphs, who beneath the pergolas rejoiced with their applause and songs and sweet harmony for the prey that winged and rash Cupid — keener-sighted than Lynceus and eye-covered Argus — was vigorously ready with his weapons to take in triumph: after a little hour had been spent by the mother goddess, and the celestial sounds and songs were broken off a while into silence, to both of us, with divine eloquence and soothing charm, in a gentle address, that most holy mouth uttered such sweet-speaking words — words shrewd and beyond doubt able to lull to sleep the wakeful guardian of the fatal treasure of Colchis, to call back to a kindly shape Aglauros the daughter of Cecrops, to restore Daphnis of Ida to human form and to the grateful flock, and to recall Cadmus and Hermione from the hissing voice and the scaled body — to Polia thus saying:

Lovely Polia, my worshipper, your holy libations, your assiduous observances —

Le quale debino dignificarte, et in questo tuo celeberrimo amore elegantemente honorarte. Da quello loco chiamate ancora vacando dagli cantici sui et soni. Hor non lasciate costei unquantulo di mora sencia vui, aciò che essa, cum aequa legie il suo Poliphilo amando, cum herculano nodo vivi, ornata del più scitissimo, et bellissimo amore, che unque nel z iii

your religious ministrations propitiating me, have made you worthy of our sweetest and most fruitful graces. And for your sincere, suppliant and unspotted offerings and solemn ceremonies, commended to my appeasing, and with a devoted heart and observant novitiate praying, I am willing to incline myself, kind and favouring and munificently bountiful, your preserver — and that your own companion Poliphilo, standing here glowing with your love, be likewise numbered among the true and happy lovers; and cleansed here of the plebeian and vulgar filth, and if by chance he was chastened of any foul defilement, let him be purified, expiated, sprinkled with my dew; and let him be unceasingly devoted to you, and prompt and assiduous to your gentle desires, and let him refuse nothing of your will. And loving one another equally, of your own accord and with wholest consent, you shall both serve my amorous fires and increase them; and for the rest of your life, protected under my keeping, you shall live blessed and glorious and exulting.

Now, so that so great a love of yours may have a happy outcome, Poliphilo, I will give and consign to you four illustrious little maidens, and dower you with their adorned virtues, well suited to your excellent spirit and generous love; and with them be you a keeper more steadfast than firm Picus toward his Canens, and a worshipper of Polia.

And without delay, calling to her from the pergolas the excellent nymph Enosina, she said to her: take with you the girl, matchless Monori, and watchful Phrontis and her silent sister Critoa, and be inseparable companions of this our athlete and loving servant of Polia. And by my fated command be the cause that both of them are of equal and mutual affection. And without pause she drew from within the oyster-shell two rings, each with a precious violet gem set turning in it, and gave one to Polia and the other to me, with most eloquent command that we should endure forever adorned with such a divine gift, and should keep her edict deep and always hidden, with a serene brow and a kindly, soothing face.

And turning in a moment, to you, Polia, she said: likewise I present four other noble and most accomplished maidens into your unceasing company, who shall make you worthy and elegantly honour you in this most celebrated love of yours.

Calling them too from that place, at leisure from their songs and sounds, she charged Adiacorista with the three noble sisters, Pistinia, Sophrosyne and Edosia, saying: now let these not leave her for the least while without you, so that she, loving her Poliphilo by equal law, may live bound in a Herculean knot, adorned with the most accomplished and most beautiful love that ever in her —

Notes

Confidence: medium. Venus's speech is long, formal, and syntactically tangled in the original, and English has to break its periods; the sense is secure throughout. The nymphs' names are Colonna's Greek coinages for the virtues each personifies (below), and rendering them as names rather than translating them is a deliberate choice — as with the sense-nymphs, the reader is meant to decode them.

  • Picus and Canens — Picus, king of Latium, so faithful to his wife Canens

  • Venus's charge continues onto p. 366.

suo seculo celebre, et di memorato dignio se sentisse. Al genio indulgendo et nunque defraudando. Offerentise hostia iniuge cum sincera et mera fede verso a lui, et labante firmare, anxio et solicito beneficamente recevere, et gratificabonda, cum tenacissimo vinculo amplexare. Tutte queste dive Nymphe lo imperio dilla suprana dea sencia inducie incominciorono ciascuna il consignato, et credito suo amorosamente cum hianti osculi consaviare in stricti amplexi, cum molti Nymphaei blandimenti et attractive charitie, et promiscuamente alacre, et saepicule praessulamente amplexandone saviate, cum debite et conveniente inclinatione annuendo alla Divina Matre, il credito overo commisso principiorono sedule et cum praecipua comitate a famulare. Et al promptissimo filiolo, non prima quasi le sancte parolette prolate, et finito il divo rationamento, che lui cum genuina licentia procace sencia pietate severo, non della gortiniaca, ma dilla volante sagitta d’oro non dal Ithyreo arco ma divino directa et non più praesto dal impeto dilla rigente cordula lo amento fue expulso, che per medio trasvecta dil mio dissaveduto core repente confixe. Et d’indi properamente cusì cruorosa tincta nel mio inflammato pecto, et dal caldo cruore fumidula uscitene del sagittario vulnere, non risanabile dal trago frutice cretense. Et sencia morula trasfixe, et quello dilla mia pyrrothricha Polia, et nel suo palpitante pecto dilla propria illibata alma retincto, ello la vulnerante et cruentata sagitta riassumpse, et di subito nel materno fonte lavatola la repose. O me che incontinente di sentire principiai essere nelle penetrale, et intime viscere le mellite uredine di una exuberante flammula diffusa, et lernicamente disseminata, et tutto me occupare et d’amorosi ardori obducto tremiculo, et di offuscare gli ochii meii. Et sencia pausula lo incenso pecto reseratose ad sé più harpylatica et mordacemente attraxe, che le anguinee thriche del Polypo, et che il typhone sorbiculabundo l’aqua, et intromisso l’amore pretioso et il divulo effigiato di Polia nunque obliterabile, cum le ingenue caste et dulcissime conditione nel praeparato et amorosamente disposito subiecto informantise, ove aeternalmente dominabonda rimanse, et quel coelico, et inelluibile simulachro firmatissimamente impresso speciosissimo. Quale arefacta et siccissima palea rimasto in subitaneo et violente foco adusta, et come facole di arsibile teda, non servata capillare particula, che d’amorosa flamma non penetrasse. Et quasi ad me parve d’altra forma acconciamente immutarme. Cum grande vacillamento et carivatione dillo intellecto di non valer comprehendere se non per quale assimilitudine che Hermaphrodito cum Salmace nel vivo, et fresco fonte amplexantise advene, quando elli nel promiscuo sexo

— famous age she might feel herself, and worthy of memory; indulging her genius and never defrauding it. Offering herself a yoked sacrifice with sincere and pure faith toward him, and steadying what would falter, anxiously and carefully and kindly to receive him, and gratefully, with the most tenacious bond, to embrace him.

All these divine nymphs, at the command of the sovereign goddess and without delay, each began lovingly to kiss with open kisses the one committed and entrusted to her, in close embraces, with many nymphish blandishments and attractive endearments; and promiscuously eager, and often pressingly embracing and kissing us, with due and fitting inclination assenting to the divine Mother, they began assiduously and with especial courtesy to serve the charge committed to them.

And to the most prompt little son — the holy little words scarcely uttered and the divine discourse hardly finished — he, with native licence, bold and pitiless and severe, with the flying golden arrow (not the Gortynian, and drawn not from the Ityraean bow but the divine one), no sooner had the string's [?loosing] driven it out with the force of the taut cord than it struck, carried through the middle of my unwitting heart, and suddenly pierced it; and then, so quickly, dyed with gore, smoking from the hot blood issuing out of the arrow-wound — a wound not to be healed by the Cretan dittany shrub — without delay it transfixed also the heart of my red-haired Polia, and in her throbbing breast, stained with her own unspotted soul, he took the wounding and bloodied arrow up again, and at once, washing it in the maternal fountain, laid it back.

Ah me, that straightway I began to feel in the innermost and deepest bowels the honeyed burning of an exuberant little flame, spread and sown far and wide, and occupying all of me; and clouded over with amorous ardours I trembled, and my eyes were darkened. And without pause my kindled breast, opening to itself, drew her to it more harpy-like and more bitingly than the snaky hair of the polyp, than the whirlwind swallowing water; and the precious love and the divine and never-to-be-blotted image of Polia being let in — with its noble, chaste and sweetest conditions, informing the prepared and lovingly disposed subject where it remained eternally ruling, that heavenly and unwashable likeness most firmly imprinted and most beautiful — I remained like dry and parched straw kindled in a sudden and violent fire, and like the torches of burnable pine, not a hair's particle preserved that the amorous flame did not penetrate. And it almost seemed to me that I was fittingly changed into another form, with great wavering and faintness of the intellect, unable to comprehend it except by that likeness of Hermaphroditus embracing Salmacis in the living, fresh spring, when he, in the mingled sex —

Dunque morando io già nelle dulcissime flamme nun più vivo che mortuo sencia vivido pulso. Et da non impedito meato, da summa dulcitudine permitteva liberamente et exalare il spiritulo mio pensando che da Epilipsia, cum genu replicato cadendo fusce stato invaso. La pientissima Dea repente cum la diva vola lacunata, deposita la ostrea stringendo la intervallatura degli longiusculi digiti, dille salsule aque exhaurite divinamente supra nui humectando asperse. Non quale la indignabonda Diana, il sfortunato venatore imbrificò dilacerando a cani in belva vertite, ma sencia haesitare per lo opposito imbrefacto transmutando alle sacre Nymphe gratificabondo et amplexando. Né più praesto benignamente facto hebbe, et io di rore marino asperso et delibuto, che in me immediate excitati gli clarificati spiriti furono più intelligibili. Et sencia praestolatione se convertirono nel pristino stato li adusti et concremati membri et me senza fallire di digne qualitate ricentarme sentendo. Conobi veramente per assimile modo Eson rinovato non essere, né altramente in me regresso mi apparve, che alla optata luce il virbio Hippolyto revocato per grande precatione di Diana cum la herba glycyside ad la appetibile vita. Et a me affectuosamente le plebarie toge dalle assignate Nymphe exute, di candida et lautiuscula veste di novo me officiosamente vestirono. Et facti tranquillamente del nostro amoroso et corroborato stato securi, et iucundissimamente rifocillati consolabondi et di gaudio subitario et laetitia commoti et delibuti repente ne feceron cum mustei osculamenti, et cum linguario vibramine suavemente basiare et strictamente amplexare. Et cum simile modo l’uno et l’altro le iucunde et festose Nymphe, nel suo sacro collegio novo tirocinio et officio dilla foecunda natura recevendo nui tutte dulcicule lepidamente ne basiorono. Diqué la Dea genitrice, cum elegantissimo affamine, et placido colloquio et cum maiestale obtuto propitiata ratiocinando, et cum divino flato spirante geniale Balsamo dispensando cose illicite di propalatione et agli vulgari homini, non di relato effabile, dando opera diutine di stabilire, et di fermentare gli nostri accensi amori, et di unire unanimi gli nostri cori sotto alle sue fructuose et dolce legie cum extento aevo, et negli stabili et parili amori magnanimi essere ne fece. Et per tanto sempre pia di porgere et munificamente favore conferire et munimine ad omni occurrente perturbatione ultronea et largamente promisse, et in tale colloquio mitissima la gratia sua lepidissimamente conferendo. Advene dunque che uno viriato milite nell’aspecto divo giù per gli z iiii

— is seen to transform into a single form. Neither more nor less than unhappy Byblis, feeling her own tears become for her a liquid fountain of the Naiad nymphs. Dwelling then already in the sweetest flames, no longer alive than dead, without a living pulse — and by an unimpeded passage, out of the height of sweetness, I freely let my little spirit exhale, thinking I had been seized by epilepsy, falling with buckled knee.

The most merciful goddess, suddenly, with her cupped divine palm — having set down the oyster-shell and closing the spaces of her longish fingers — divinely drew up the salt waters and, moistening, sprinkled them over us. Not as indignant Diana rained upon the unlucky hunter, tearing him to a beast for the dogs, but on the contrary, unhesitating, sprinkling to transmute — gratifying and embracing the sacred nymphs. And no sooner had she kindly done it, and I was sprinkled and anointed with the sea-dew, than in me at once the clarified spirits were stirred and grew more intelligible; and without waiting my burnt and consumed members reverted to their former state, and I felt myself, without fail, renewed with worthy qualities. I knew truly that I was renewed in a like manner to Aeson, nor did my return to myself seem otherwise than when twice-living Hippolytus was called back to the longed-for light, by Diana's great prayer, with the herb glycyside, to desirable life. And the assigned nymphs affectionately stripped from me the plebeian robes, and dutifully clothed me anew in a white and somewhat elegant garment. And being made tranquilly secure of our amorous and strengthened state, and most delightfully refreshed, consoled, and moved and anointed with sudden joy and gladness, at once they made us kiss with new-wine kisses, and with the vibration of the tongue sweetly to kiss and closely to embrace. And in like manner both of us, the joyful and festive nymphs, in their sacred college receiving us to the new novitiate and office of fruitful nature, all sweetly and charmingly kissed.

Whereupon the mother goddess, with most elegant address and placid discourse, and propitious with a majestic gaze, reasoning, and with divine breath dispensing a genial balsam — things unlawful to publish, and to vulgar men not to be told — labouring long to establish and to ferment our kindled loves, and to unite our hearts as one under her fruitful and sweet laws for a long age, made us great-souled in stable and equal love. And therefore, ever devoted to offering and munificently conferring favour and defence against every chance perturbation, she largely and freely promised; and in such discourse, most gentle, most charmingly conferring her grace.

Cum prominente crista et insigne apice aureo sopra il cono induto di thorace d’oro. Quale il divo Iulio di Britannia non portoe, né dedicoe alla genitrice nel suo templo. Etquivi ello et essa, non cum humani blandimenti et charitie, ma cumdivini gesti et affecto se implicatamente amplexavano cum innodanti abraciamenti. Per la quale cosa le Nymphe questo advertente, cum humile et reverente parlare licentia impetrorono, et io ancora, et la mia activa Polia il simigliante facendo, cum quel modo possibile, che allhora nui potevamo le immortale gratie dicte, ne dispartissemo. Rimanseron dunque solamente cum la divina Matre e cum il filio, et cum li continuamente al fonte circumastanti, et il venuto armigero ad divini et solatiosi oblectamenti abiecti tutti gli amiculi.* * ** ***

— steps, out of the recesses beneath the first pergola, valiantly making for the sacred fountain, with a majestic and vehement face, and breasted and stout- hearted with formidable ferocity, brawny and tall with broad shoulders, with the lights of his eyes keen and terrible, but of a venerable dignity. Sumptuously and proudly and divinely adorned, with a most cunningly wrought silver-gleaming shield such as Brontes and his companions did not make for the exiled Trojan; his head covered with a shining helmet wreathed with olive flowers, with a prominent crest and a noble golden apex above the cone; clad in a golden cuirass, such as the divine Julius did not bring from Britain nor dedicate to his mother in her temple; nor did the excellent craftsman Didymaon ever make such a one; with a pendent baldric, or cross-belt, girt about him, with a most sumptuous golden scimitar knotted to it; with all the most beautiful military accoutrements, most robustly holding a scourge in his hand, and attended by his growling Lycaon.

Come now at last to the delightful and delicious fountain, he showed himself eager to lay down his armour and, disarmed, to go in to the beloved goddess. And there he and she embraced one another intricately, not with human blandishments and endearments but with divine gestures and affection, in knotting embraces. Whereupon the nymphs, perceiving this, with humble and reverent speech obtained leave, and I too, and my lively Polia doing the like, in what manner we then could, having spoken our thanks to the immortal graces, departed. There remained, then, only the divine Mother with her son, and with those continually standing about the fountain, and the armed one newly come, cast down to divine and solacing delights — all the attendants withdrawn.

The polyandrion — the ruined temple and its tombs.

PER LO ADVENTO DIL ARMIGERO RECENSENTE POLIPHILO NARRA, CHE FORA DIL THEATRO USCIRONO CUM TUTTO IL CONSORTIO, ET CUM L’ALTRE NYMPHE AD UNO SACRO FONTE PERVENERON. OVE LE NYMPHE NARRANO DEL SEPULCHRO DI ADONE. ET COME LA DEA ANNIVERSARIAMENTE IVI CONVENIVA ADIMPIRE LE SANCTE CERIMONIE. ET CESSANDO DAL TRIPUDIO ET CANTARE SUASENO. POSCIA A POLIA CHE ELLA NARRASSE LA SUA ORIGINE, ET IL SUO INAMORARE.

ORNATO DI NOVELLE QUALITATE CUM la mia praestante Polia, et cum le comite nostre, d’indi al sacro fonte dispartendo nui, per quella medesima porta et adito che nui intrassemo fora similmente retornassimo ove praestolavano tutte le Nymphe dagli suavi soni et cantatrice. Le quale inseme cum nui festive veneron, onde io farcito di fructuoso amore nell’accenso pecto ampliato, cum omni accessoria dulcedine, exclusi gli praeteriti dolori, extincto omni molesto contrario, refrenato omni ancipite cogitato, già più non haesitante di Polia, ma come ad unica Augusta de l’alma mia. Silvia del core mio, et di la vita Ptolomaea. Arsacida degli sensi, et Murana del mio amore, et de me tutto patrona et reverenda Imperatrice festivo et laeto humilmente, et deditissimo contento succumbo, cum più intenso, sincero, et honorario amore, che non fece il pio Imperatore alla sua cara bella et diva Adultera. Hogi mai adepto il suo pretioso amore, et spontale core, in questo amoroso certamine Aristeo. Advene che la facetissima turbula delle Nymphe, a pristini solatii, et piaceri retornorono, cum le coeleste harmonie, et angelici concenti, cum iochi puellari et solatiosi scherci, et alacre ridibonde monstrantise dilli nostri obtenuti et consumati desii, blande dantise circa nui in gestivo circinao. Et per la sancta insula, per le itione overo strate definite per le plante degli pomiferi horti, le quale erano virente di perenne et vernea z v

By the coming of the armed one just recounted, Poliphilo tells how they went out of the theatre with all the company, and with the other nymphs came to a sacred fountain; where the nymphs tell of the sepulchre of Adonis, and how the goddess yearly assembled there to fulfil the holy ceremonies; and, ceasing from the dance and song, they urged Polia that she should tell her origin and her falling in love.

Adorned with new qualities, with my excellent Polia and with our companions, departing thence from the sacred fountain, we returned out likewise by that same gate and entrance by which we had come in, where all the nymphs of the sweet sounds and singing awaited. Who came festively together with us; whereupon I, stuffed with fruitful love in my kindled and enlarged breast, with every accessory sweetness, shut out my past sorrows, every troublesome contrary extinguished, every doubtful thought curbed, no longer now hesitating over Polia, but as to the sole Augusta of my soul. Sylvia of my heart, and the Ptolemaïs of my life. Arsacid of my senses, and Myrrha of my love, and of all of me the mistress and revered Empress — festive and glad, humbly and most devotedly, I contentedly succumb, with a more intense, sincere and honourable love than pious Aeneas bore to his dear, beautiful and divine mistress. Having now at last won her precious love and willing heart, in this amorous contest an Aristaeus.

It came to pass that the merry little throng of nymphs returned to their former solaces and pleasures, with the celestial harmonies and angelic concord, with girlish games and solacing jests, and showing themselves briskly full of laughter at our obtained and consummated desires, gently giving themselves around us in a sporting round. And through the holy island, by the paths or streets marked out among the plants of the fruit-bearing orchards, which were green with perennial and spring —

— foliage, walled about with box like little walls, and with myrtle and juniper, ten paces long-wise, alternating, and hanging steeply the other way. Beyond, then, these described enclosures were exceeded by two paces of elegant latticework of marble, a sixth of a foot [thick?], with a symmetrical square columniation suitably distributed and placed, with every excellent requisite. These had their openings figured as roses and rhombs, and with a lovely allotment of a reddening colour like bright cinnabar. Through which, vine-like, wound rose-bushes of manifold colouring and foliage.

Through these aforesaid places, both holding hands, they began to lead, pleasantly urging Polia that — in the manner in which for them and for her there were little flowering wreaths pressed round like blond crowns — she likewise should gather up the scattered flowers for me, and lovingly compose, threading them, a jewel. And there, with great solace and excess of pleasure, some of the divine nymphs, attending the gathering, placidly inclined themselves with my much-loved Polia, and helped. Who without pause, easily compelled by the amorous rules, with her ready and most apt hands, with quickened delight began composedly to weave the circular Polycarpia of the various fragrant little flowers; and, uprooting from her copious head the golden and very long hairs — glittering like the finest spun thread of pure gold, which, stretched out in a long series flowing down her chaste back, waved beautifully — she busily set about binding the gathered heaped flowers. And with such pleasure and sport of sweet flame, most glad and untiring, we dallied. Now through flowery meadows, now through greenest little woods, hedged with irrigating channels and trembling brooks; now through the sweet shades of the tree-set walks covered with flowering periwinkle, with the crown of topiary work — and the fame of the place and the clemency of the sky, without the trouble of showers and heat, drawing us on with a voluptuous invitation, provoking and alluring — joyfully we came to a most limpid and sacred fountain flowing from a broad spring. Not with mossy, hairy-mossed, maidenhair- and-spleenwort banks, but its banks were hedged about and adorned with borders of Macedonian marble, not red, but of itself lustrous and veined, decorated with the shade of riverside shoots, with manifold offspring of flower of various and pleasant fragrance, and their freshest and dewy fragrant leaves disposed.

Per sotto del quale arboramento di trunchi directissimi immuni passo uno di impedienti rami, cernivasi bellamente il libero aire degli contermini. Nel comspecto del introito al fonte, era una perguletta contermina, lata quanto una facia della fontana, tra angulo ad angulo, et levata altro tanto, uno passo al perpendiculo, et uno al flexo consignato. — temperate shades breathed, mingled with reddening cornel-cherries copious with their fruit, and with a fresh-sprouting cone-shaped little cypress-grove, and a lofty palm-grove, and a poplar, and a resinous and cone-bearing pine-grove — set in order with composed distance and mutual arrangement, and distributed in a circle about this fated fountain, and adorned and decorated with a clothing of flower and leaf — covered the shaggy ground with soft and finest little pot-herbs, like a carpet of fresh shade. Beneath which planting of straightest trunks, clear of a single obstructing branch, the free air of the neighbouring parts could be beautifully seen through.

To which sacred fountain, holy, shaped as a hexagon and twelve paces in its surrounding measure, the inner tree-set enclosure stood off from the containing edge of the fountain — that is, from the marble borders — by four paces; and in circumference, thirty-six paces. It was all of orange, lemon and citron trees, affording a pleasant and most placid enclosure, or cloister; offering to the eyes it granted a most admirable show, of thick density, of festive leaves and fragrant flowers — a most pleasing picture, russet, or vermilion mortified in yellow, of the ripened fruits, and excellently shining with a thick offspring, one tree to another, composedly matched in an alternating marriage, uniform, full of every singing bird — chiefly of nightingales, of thrushes, and of solitary blackbirds, with delightful expressions, urged to the sweet warble by the amorous impulse of the spring season.

And there, at the round trunks of the trees just recounted, a lattice-fence artfully hedged them, raised a foot high, surrounding in a circle, of manifold- devised open interlacing, of red erythraean wood, of sandalwood; in which were excellently woven and intertwined rose-bushes of the hundred-petalled kind, little Greek autumn ones, and small crowns of purpling flower straying twined along the lattice; with leaves not to fall, of unthinkable fragrance, of most admirable freshness.

Here, by a little postern of the same aforesaid work, we religiously entered. In sight of the entrance to the fountain there was a little pergola adjoining, as wide as one face of the fountain, angle to angle, and raised as much — one pace to the perpendicular, and one to the assigned bend. Twelve feet long, roofed with most noble rose-bushes teeming with vermilion flower, with their most delightful fragrance, carried fittingly upon little rods of gleaming gold; with the bright stone floor, or tessellated and inlaid pavement wrought vermiculate of precious little stones. And close to the sides of the pergola stood seats of jasper —

Il quale per omni parte virente sencia alcuna denudatione era, et tutto di minutissimo serpillo olente caesariato, del quale uno solo folio al altro non superava, cum gratifica densatura fina alle praeripie del fonte vernante contegendo cum aequaria tonsura. Quivi sotto mirai una veneranda factura. La quale queste dive Nymphe et nui divotamente reverissemo uno miraveglioso et di mysterio pleno stava deputato tale sepulchro pedi quinarii in longitudine, et in latitudine dextante altra tanto levato excluso il socco, cum la coronicetta, che era quincuncio, il quale tumulo disseron le Nymphe essere del venatore Adone, in quel loco dal dentato Apro interempto. Et in questo loco etiam similmente la Sancta Venere uscendo di questo fonte nuda, in quelli rosarii lancinovi la divina Sura, per soccorrere quello dal zelotypo Marte verberato cum vultuosa facia et indignata, et cum angore d’animo. Questa tale historietta se vedea perfectamente inscalpta in uno lato per longo del sepulchro. Et il filio Cupidine recolgiere poscia il purpurissimo sangue in uno cortice di Ostrea. Subiungendo che quel divino cruore era reposito in quel sepulchro, cum il cinere, cum omni sancto rito collocato. Diqué nel fronte del nostro ingresso del sepulchro era excavato circularmente per il capto del quadrato, et obturato, poscia di petra pretiosa di Iacyntho, di colore vermiglio transparente, cum grande corruscatione di flammeo splendore, per il lume opposito instabile ardendo, che apena valeva io gli ochii per il vacillamento affirmare. Dal altro lato per il longo del sepulchro vidi similmente Adone, cum alcuni pastori venatore caelati, tra alquanti arbusculi, cum cani et il morto Apro, et esso da quello occiso. Et Venere dolorosamente lachrymabunda negli pietosi amplexi di tre Nymphe semianime cadeva, di subtilissimo panno indute inseme cum la dea collachrymavano. Et il filio cum uno fasciculo di rose gli ochii materni vidi di liquante lachryme plorabundo tergente. Quivi tra uno et l’altro sexo in una corolla di myrto vidi cusì inscripto. IMPURA SUAVITAS. Per tale modo ne l’altra historietta in graeco cusì era expresso. ADONIA Tanto tutte queste cose exquisitamente di sculptura ficte se praestavano, che io me commovi in una dolcecia di pietate. Il quadrato dunque opposito a quello del lume perpendicularmente derivava

— material, and of fitting most elegant lineaments marked out, seven-twelfths and a half-as in the seat, upon the flat.

The tessellated pavement was laid level over all the enclosed ground. Which was on every side green without any bareness, and all tressed with tiniest fragrant thyme, of which a single leaf did not stand above another, with a gratifying denseness clothing right up to the banks of the fountain, springlike, with an even shearing.

Here beneath I beheld a venerable work, which these divine nymphs and we devoutly reverenced. A marvellous sepulchre, full of mystery, stood there appointed: five feet in length, and in breadth as much raised — ten inches — excluding the socle, with the little cornice, which was a quincunx; which tomb the nymphs said to be of the hunter Adonis, slain in that place by the tusked boar. And in this place likewise the holy Venus, coming naked out of this fountain, tore in those rose-bushes her divine calf, to succour him who had been beaten by jealous Mars, with a woeful and indignant face, and with anguish of soul.

This little history was seen perfectly carved on one long side of the sepulchre; and the son Cupid then gathering the purple blood into an oyster-shell — adding that that divine gore was laid up in that sepulchre, placed with the ash, with every holy rite. Whereupon on the front of our entrance to the sepulchre it was hollowed out in a circle to the size of the square, and then stopped up with the precious stone jacinth, of a transparent vermilion colour, with great coruscation of flaming splendour, burning unsteadily by the light set opposite, so that I could scarcely fix my eyes on it for the wavering.

On the other long side of the sepulchre I saw likewise Adonis, carved with some shepherd-hunters, among some little shrubs, with dogs and the dead boar, and he slain by it; and Venus falling sorrowfully weeping into the pitiful embraces of three half-swooning nymphs, clothed in finest cloth, weeping together with the goddess; and the son, with a little bundle of roses, I saw wiping the maternal eyes, tearful with flowing tears.

There, between the one sex and the other, in a garland of myrtle, I saw thus inscribed: IMPURA SUAVITAS. In like manner on the other little history, in Greek, was thus expressed: ΑΔΩΝΙΑ. So exquisitely feigned in sculpture did all these things present themselves, that I was moved to a sweetness of pity.

— above the fountain. In which was aptly fixed a golden serpent, feigned to creep out from a hidden cleft of rock, which, with coiled windings of a fitting thickness, vomited abundantly into the sonorous fountain the clearest water. So that by this mastery the meaning craftsman had cast the serpent in a globed coil, to curb the force of the water — which, by a free and straight pipe, would have scattered beyond the limits of the fountain.

Upon the flat of the aforesaid sepulchre the divine Mother sat, carved as a woman in childbed — not without utmost astonishment — of a precious three-coloured sardonyx stone, upon an antique seat, her seat not exceeding the sardine vein; but with incredible invention and artifice the whole little Cytherean body was of the milky vein of the onyx, as though unclothed, because only a veiling was left of the red vein concealing the secret of nature, veiling part of one thigh, and the rest descending over the flat, passing then up over —

Notes

Confidence: medium. The cameo-carving conceit is the difficulty: Venus is carved from a banded three-colour sardonyx so that the layers of the stone supply the flesh (white onyx vein), the drapery (red vein), and the seat (dark sardine vein). The description of which vein does what is dense and continues onto p. 374; the sense is secure but the exact anatomy of the drapery is intricate.

— the left nipple, resisting it. And from the shoulders, turned to the water, hanging down, it showed — imitating with marvellous sculpture, nonetheless, the holy members — she embracing and giving suck to Cupid, indicating with the image the maternal affection, with a gracious colouring of the cheeks of both from the reddening vein, with her right little hand [?patting]. O most beautiful work, miraculous to contemplate, wanting only the vital spirit. With the parted brow, the ringleted hairs over the smooth temples, and at the back of the head gathered composedly with a little knotted band; the loosed part thence extended down to the seat, tendril-like. And exactly sculpted the little tresses, with the openwork whorls egregiously expressed by the labour of the drill, preserved of the translucent shining sardine vein — such as, of happy Polycrates, enclosed in the golden horn, was not dedicated by Augustus in the shrine of Concord. The left little foot she held drawn back to the seat, and the other stretched to the outer edge or rim of the flat. Which holy foot the nymphs, bowed and fallen kneeling, and we, kissed with utmost religion. Beneath which little foot, in the little cornice, there remained a small band of lineament clear; and on this, in little letters of our own, I saw annotated this distich:

Non lac saeve puer, lachrymas sed sugis amaras,
Reddendas matri, carique Adonis amore.

Not milk, cruel boy, but bitter tears you suck,
to be given back to your mother, for love of dear Adonis.

<!--[Iscrizione nella figura:] IMPURA SUAVITAS-->

Facto et peracta debitamente questa honoraria et divota cerimonia, fora uscissimo della sacrata perguletta. Le inclyte Nymphe cum affabile facundia a noi disseron. Sapiate che il praesente loco è mysterioso, et di maximo venerato celeberrimo, et in omni anno anniversariamente il pridiano dì delle calende di Magio, veni quivi la Divina matre, cum il dilecto filio cum divina pompa di lustratione, et cum essa tute nui sue subdite, et al suo imperio ultronee cum observato famulitio, et cum superba solemnitate convenimo. Pervenuta dunque quivi cum suave lachryme et suspiruli. Da nui impera, che tutte le rose della pergula, et denudati, ancora gli cancelli di quelle siano, et sopra il sepulchro alabastriceo, cum invocatione altisone ritualmente spargere, et congestitiamente coprire. Poscia cum il dicto ordine, et processo primo se parte. Nel sequente dì calendario gli spoliati rosarii se reflorano al numero di rose candente. Et ad gli idi un’altra fiata la dea cum il modo primo retornando. E

Having done and duly performed this honorary and devout ceremony, we went out of the sacred little pergola. The illustrious nymphs with affable eloquence said to us: Know that the present place is mysterious, and most celebrated with the greatest veneration; and every year, anniversarily, on the day before the Kalends of May, the divine Mother comes here with her beloved son in a divine pomp of lustration, and with her all we her subjects, of our own accord at her command, with observed attendance and with proud solemnity, assemble. Come here, then, with sweet tears and little sighs, she commands us that all the roses of the pergola — and the lattices too be stripped of them — and over the alabaster sepulchre, with high-sounding invocation, ritually to strew them, and heapingly to cover it. Then in the said order and process she first departs. On the following calendar day the despoiled rose-bushes reflower to the same number of glowing roses.

And on the Ides another time the goddess, returning in the former manner —

Daposcia che la divina Domina solitata nel fonte lavatose, et d’indi uscita, iterato in commemoratione et memoriale amoroso dil batuto caro et dilecto Adone da Marte, cum gli ochii succidi al sepulchro super iniecta amplexabonda cum lachryme emanante le rosee gene cohumidulante et tutte nui cum pipatione lamentabile pietosamente plora, perché in tale dì la diva sura, del pedusculo da nui deosculato, da gli spini di queste rose se punxe. Et perciò in tale giorno medesimo solemnemente se resera da essa et revelato el coperculo de il sancto reposito, et cum veneranda cerimonia tutte nui laete et exultante et cantante. Il filio ricevuto porta il cortice de l’ostrea cum il divino cruore. Et lei antista, et novissima gerula del fasciculo delle rose immote del suo virore, cum serena venustate festivissima. Non più praesto il pretioso liquore è fora extracto, che repente tutte le bianchissime rose, como al praesente appareno in purpureo colore se retingono. Et cum tale ordine tre fiate pomposamente lustrando questo fonte et lei sola lachrymabile gli ochii madenti, cum il manipulo rosario si terge, alla circinatione terna, le cose sacrale reposite in suo loco, tutto quel celeberrimo dì solemnemente solo a piaceri, chori, soni, et cantilatione è dispensato dicatissimo. Et in tale dì facilmente la gratia sua s’impetra. All’incontro del sepulchro al fonte, erano cinque graduli della petra limitata, proclivanti fina al piano fondo, non scruposo, non glareaceo, ma di pretiosa, et vermiculata sectilatione silicato. L’alveo del rivoletto emissario emanava, subterraneo fina ultra gli cancelli la successiva aqua. Domesticamente havendo le celibe Nymphe facondamente narrato tanto memorando et sì curioso mysterio, incominciorono iterum a sonare, et rithmiticamente le recensite historiole, et transacti casi suavissimamente et cum maxima voluptate a cantilare. Et in gyro della fontana chorizante per lunga mora poscia tutti geniculatamente sedenti et complicite, in tanta quam acceptissima amoenitate, et iucundissima virentia. Io allhora peculiarmente dispoliato et exempto di omni retrahente respecto, in quella insueta redolentia della mia abrodieta Polia, che ancora da essa lautissima et mundiciante spirava, et da una recente exalatione di quella fragrante nitella de gli delicati habiti sui roranti balsamo tutto perfuso, nel suo gremio me amorosa, et licentemente collocai, ardelio basiando, et le lactee mano, et quel pecto niveo, di lustrario, eburneo illucente, et poscia mutuamente, non ingrato ma aemulario volupticamente essendo nel conspecto suo tali effecti impulsi d’amore approbavano, per la quale cosa le sonatrice supra la gratissima virentia

— she says that devoutly the heap of roses is removed from the sepulchre, and within the fountain, with divine applause, all scattered; and, thrown into the emissary rivulet, thence they are carried away.

After the divine Lady, as is her custom, has washed herself in the fountain and come out of it, again in commemoration and amorous memorial of the dear and beloved Adonis, beaten by Mars, with moist eyes she throws herself embracing over the sepulchre, weeping, her rosy cheeks bedewed with flowing tears; and all we, with a lamentable piping, piteously weep, because on such a day the divine calf — of the little foot we kissed — was pricked by the thorns of these roses. And therefore on that same day the cover of the holy repository is solemnly unsealed by her and revealed, and with venerable ceremony all we, glad and exulting and singing — the son having received it, carries the oyster-shell with the divine gore, and she, the priestess and newest bearer of the bundle of roses unwilted of their greenness, with serene grace, most festive.

No sooner is the precious liquor drawn out than at once all the whitest roses, as at present they appear, are re-dyed in purple colour. And in such order, three times pompously lustrating this fountain, and she alone weeping, her eyes wet, wipes them with the handful of roses; at the third circling, the sacred things laid back in their place, all that most celebrated day is solemnly dispensed to pleasures alone, to dances, sounds and songs, most dedicated. And on such a day her grace is easily obtained.

Opposite the sepulchre, at the fountain, there were five steps of the bordering stone, sloping down to the flat bottom — not rough, not gravelly, but of precious and vermiculate inlaid paving. The channel of the emissary rivulet flowed on, underground, beyond the lattices, carrying the succeeding water.

The maiden nymphs having domestically and eloquently narrated so memorable and so curious a mystery, began again to sound, and rhythmically, most sweetly and with the greatest pleasure, to sing the recounted little histories and the past happenings. And dancing in a circle about the fountain for a long while, then all kneeling and seated and interlaced, in such most acceptable pleasantness and most delightful greenery — I then, particularly stripped and freed of every holding- back respect, in that unwonted fragrance of my dainty Polia, which still breathed from her most elegant and cleanly self, and being all perfumed with a recent exhalation of that fragrant sweetness of her delicate garments dewing balsam — I placed myself amorously and freely in her lap, eagerly kissing both her milky hands and that snowy breast, ivory and lustrous shining; and then mutually — not ungrateful but voluptuously vying — being in her sight, such impulses of love approved themselves. Whereupon the singers, set out over the most pleasing greenery —

expositise et gli melodi strumenti. Et le concentore negli sui deliciosi pecti le melliflue voce reservate se tacitorono. Diqué cusì alacre et voluptuosamente ociante cum Nympheo confabulamento per aliquantula mora monstroronse molto cupide il nostro stato et conditione intendere. Et una tra l’altre nominata Polyorimene summamente faceta et placivola dixe. O Polia consortiale nostra, et partiaria conservula della colenda genitrice la venustate dell’aspecto tuo elegante et forma conspicua et insigne, et ingenue tue praeclare virtute et praestante mente morigerata, et la tua praecipua, et incomparabile bellecia, rendino nui non immeritamente avide de intendere degli vostri foelici amori la cagione, et lo initio originale della tua egregia et generosa progenie. La quale essere arbitramo notabile et sublime et di praeclaro exordio. Imperò che comporto havemo te di probitate, di ingegnio, et di literatura non mediocre erudita. Di solertia insignita, cum praecipua gratia negli virginei gesti di eximia forma. Di excedente pulchritudine, cum suavissima venustate, et di honestamento praestantissima, et summe dignanda di honore. Perché la tua spectatissima effigie et caelico simulacro extremamente bella et geniale non è totalmente terrestre, ma tuttavia cum più del divo per omni modo cum aperto indicio appare. Diciò ad nui dunque grato immo gratissimo gli molestosi affani, et gli impudenti sdegni per discorde et inaequale dispositione, et alcuna fiata fingirse sorda di non sentire le solicite precatione, degli improbi et passionati amatori. Et como uno all’altro continuamente non videntise il tristo et concupiscibile core, solamente pascono d’uno consolatorio suaso. Procedente da composite imaginatione et delectabili figmenti, quale vorebbeno, et summopere concupiscono, et di dolci suspirulamini et di simulato solamine et placere ad se medesimi vanamente satisfacendo. Et in tale commendabile ocio, et acto intente non rincresserae il nostro quieto et solacevole sedentario. *

— and their melodious instruments, and the singers, the mellifluous voices held back in their delicious breasts, fell silent.

Whereupon, thus briskly and voluptuously at leisure, with nymphish converse for a little while, they showed themselves much desirous to learn our state and condition. And one among the others, named Polyorimene, most witty and pleasing, said: O Polia, our consort and partner fellow-servant of the goddess we cultivate, the loveliness of your elegant aspect and your conspicuous and notable form, and your noble and illustrious virtues and excellent well-mannered mind, and your especial and incomparable beauty, make us not undeservedly eager to learn the cause of your happy loves, and the original beginning of your excellent and generous lineage — which we judge to be notable and sublime and of illustrious origin. For we have gathered you to be of probity, of wit, and not meanly learned in letters; distinguished in shrewdness, with especial grace in your virgin gestures, of exceptional form; of surpassing beauty, with most sweet loveliness, and most excellent in modesty, and worthy in the highest degree of honour. Because your most admirable countenance and heavenly likeness, extremely beautiful and genial, is not wholly earthly, but rather appears in every way, by open token, to have more of the divine. Therefore tell us — grateful, indeed most grateful — of the troublesome torments and the impudent disdains, through discord and unequal disposition, and how at times you feigned yourself deaf, not to hear the anxious prayers of importunate and passionate lovers; and how, the two not continually seeing one another, the sad and desiring heart feeds only on a consoling persuasion, proceeding from composed imagination and delightful figments, such as they would wish and most greatly desire, vainly satisfying themselves with sweet little sighs and feigned solace and pleasure. And in such commendable leisure, intent on the act, our quiet and solacing sitting will not grow wearisome.

Notes

Confidence: high. This is a formal set speech — the nymph Polyorimene's request that Polia tell her story — and it is clear, courtly and free of the botanical and mythological thickets of the surrounding pages.

  • The pivot of the whole book. Polyorimene's speech is the doorway from Book I
  • "the sad and desiring heart feeds only on a consoling persuasion" — a small,
  • Polyorimene — another Colonna coinage (πολυ‑, many; the second element
p. 378confidence: highverified

Finito che la Nympha cum comitate blandissima hebbe il suo benigno suaso et multo acceptissima recordatione, che la mia acrocoma Polia propera et mansuetissima levatose cum gli sui festevoli, et facetissimi simulachri, overo sembianti, et cum punicante gene, et rubente buccule da honesto et venerante rubore suffuse aptavase di volere per omni via satisfare di natura prompta ad omni virtute, et dare opera alla honesta petitione. Non che prima peroe se potesse caelare et diciò retinere alquanto che ella intrinsicamente non suspirulasse. Il quale dulcissimo suspirulo penetroe reflectendo nel intimo del mio, immo suo core, per la uniforme convenientia. Quale advene a dui parimente participati et concordi litui. Et ciascuna cum divo obtuto respecta intrepidulamente, cum quegli ludibondi et micanti ochii, da fare (omè) gli adamanti fresi in mille fragmenticuli. Cum pie et summisse voce, et cum elegantissimi gesti decentemente reverita ogni una, ritornoe al suo solatioso sedere supra il serpilaceo solo. La initiata opera sequendo sellularia. Cum accommodata

When the nymph, with most gentle courtesy, had finished her kind persuasion and most acceptable reminder, my golden-haired Polia, ready and most mild, rising with her festive and most witty images or semblances, and with cheeks flushing and rosy little lips suffused with an honest and reverent blush, made ready to satisfy in every way — of a nature prompt to every virtue — and to give effect to the honest petition. Not, however, that she could first conceal it, or hold it in at all, that she did not inwardly sigh a little; which sweetest little sigh penetrated, reflecting, into the depth of mine — nay, of her — heart, by the uniform accord, as happens to two equally shared and concordant war-trumpets. And each, gazed upon with a divine gaze, intrepidly, with those playful and flashing eyes fit to shatter (ah me) adamant into a thousand little fragments — with pious and humble voice, and with most elegant gestures becomingly revered by every one, she returned to her solacing seat upon the thyme-grown ground, following the begun work, seated. With fitting —

Notes

Confidence: high. A single flowing sentence of preparation — Polia rising, blushing, sighing, and settling to begin her tale — clear throughout. The only interpretive touch is litui (war-trumpets) as the figure for two hearts in accord, kept literal because the image (two instruments tuned alike, one sounding when the other is struck) is the point.

  • The last beat of Book I. Polia blushes, sighs once, and sits to begin. The
  • "two equally shared and concordant war-trumpets" — Colonna's simile for
  • acrocoma — golden/fair-haired at the tips (ἄκρος + κόμη), one more of the
p. 379confidence: highverified

pronuntiatione, primo facto uno pusillo dimoramento cusì limatissimamente se pose, et enucleatamente comissima ad narrare.

FINIS DEL PRIMO LIBRO DILLA HYPNEROTOMACHIA DI POLIPHILO. * *

— pronunciation, having first made a little pause, thus most polishedly she set herself, and clearly committed herself to narrate.

Chapter XXXVIII

The awakening.

1 of 1 pages englished

p. 465confidence: highdrafted

POLIPHILO QUIVI FINISSE LA SUA HYPNEROTOMACHIA, DOLENTISE DEL SOMNO CHE NON FUE PIÙ LONGO. ET CHE IL SOLE FUE INVIDIOSO FACENDO GIORNO.

TANTO INOPINABILE DELECTAMENTO surrepto, et dagli ochii mei summoto quel spirito angelico, et subtracto fora dagli somnosi membri il dolce et suave dormire evigilantime, in quel punctulo, Omè Heu me amorosi lectori, tutto indolentime per il forte stringere de quella beata imagine, et foelice praesentia, et veneranda maiestate, lassantime et deserentime tra mira dolcecia, et intensiva amaritudine. Quando dal obtuto mio, se partirono quel iocundissimo somno, et quella diva umbra interrupta et disiecta quella mysteriosa apparitione et sublata. Per le quale fue conducto et elato ad sì alti et sublimi, et penetrabili cogitamenti. Diqué per aventura il Sole de invidia agitato di cusì beato somnio, a depraedare la gloriosa nocte, como publico inimico et Sycophanta della divina Matre, cum gli illuminosi splendori subitoso vene et a dipingere di colore roseo l’albicante Aurora discussa la perpete nocte. Et illustrato et interposito il recentato die, io rimansi stipato et completissimo di dolce, et argutula fallacia. Cogitate dunque quale livore livido, alhora ello harebbe, si io realmente sentisse perfruendo gli proprii et voluptici dilecti, de cusì formosa et diva damicella, et insigne Nympha, che esso non sostene (arbitro perché cum diva non lice) a cconcedermi la longa nocte (da indignatione) di Alcmena. Heu me perché non commutoe egli uno alquantulo della sua celeritudine, cum uno pauculo di secordia, alla mia refecta quiescentia, et praeterire unotantulo il suo statuto? Et perché alhora non mi fue arrogato il Stygio somno della Pyxide della curiosa Psyches? Et quivi Philomela anteluculo flendo promeva, tra gli spinosi rubi operta, et tra boscheti pressi di opaca coma di querculi, involuti della obliquante Periclymeno le violentie dell’adultero et infido Tereo, cum canoro garrito dicendo, Tereus Tereus, eme ebiasato?sospirando emerso et absoluto dal dolce somno repentuscule me lucubrai dicendo. Vale ergo Polia.

Tarvisii cum decorissimis Poliae amore lorulis, distineretur misellus Poliphilus.

.M. CCCC. LXVII. Kalendis Maii.

Poliphilo here ends his Hypnerotomachia, grieving that the sleep was no longer, and that the Sun was envious, and made it day.

So unlooked-for a delight snatched away, that angelic spirit taken from my eyes, and the sweet and gentle sleeping drawn out of my drowsy limbs as I woke — in that instant, alas, ah me, amorous readers, I ached all over from the hard clasping of that blessed image, that happy presence, that venerable majesty, which left me and deserted me between wondrous sweetness and piercing bitterness. When from my gaze there departed that most joyful sleep and that divine shade, the mysterious apparition broken off, scattered, and carried away — by which I had been led and lifted to thoughts so high, so sublime, so piercing.

And then, as it happened, the Sun, stirred to envy by so blessed a dream, coming to plunder the glorious night like a public enemy and an informer against the divine Mother, came suddenly with his shining splendours and painted the whitening Dawn a rosy colour, the long night dispelled. And the new-made day being lit and set between us, I was left crammed and utterly filled with a sweet and subtle deception.

Consider, then, what livid spite he would have had, if I had truly felt, in the enjoying of them, the very voluptuous delights of so shapely and divine a damsel, so matchless a nymph — he who would not endure (because, I judge, with a goddess it is not permitted) to grant me the long night of Alcmena. Ah me, why did he not exchange a little of his swiftness for a little sloth, for the sake of my restored rest, and overrun his appointed hour by just so much? And why was the Stygian sleep out of curious Psyche's box not laid upon me then?

And there Philomela, weeping before daybreak, hidden among thorny brambles and in thickets close with the dark foliage of small oaks wound about with twining honeysuckle, was pouring out the violence of that adulterous and faithless Tereus, saying with tuneful chatter: Tereus, Tereus — [?have I been wronged]. Sighing, risen and released from the sweet sleep, I woke of a sudden into the small hours, saying: Farewell then, Polia.

At Treviso, where wretched Poliphilo was held fast in the most comely little thongs of Polia's love. 1467, on the Kalends of May.

Notes

Confidence: high. The narrative Italian here is unusually plain for Colonna and the classical allusions (Alcmena, Psyche's box, Philomela and Tereus) are all securely identifiable. The one weak point is the corrupt Greek noted below, which is isolated to three words and does not affect the sense of the passage.

  • eme ebiasato? — the transcription reads Tereus Tereus, eme ebiasato?
  • Omè Heu me — the Italian exclamation immediately doubled by the Latin.
  • la longa nocte di Alcmena — Jupiter stopped the sun to lengthen the night
  • Tarvisii — Treviso, where the historical Francesco Colonna's Polia is

Polia's epitaph, and the one in which Poliphilo speaks.

EPITAPHIUM POLIAE

F œlix Polia, quæ sepulta vivis C haro Marte Poliphilus quiescens I am fecit vigilare te sopitam.

EPITAPHIUM UBI POLIPHILUS LOQUITUR.

VIATOR FAC QUAESO MORULAM, POLIAE NYMPHAE HIC EST MYROPOLIUM. QUAE NAM INQUIES POLIA? FLOS ILLE OMNEM REDOLENS VIRTUTEM SPECTATISSIMUS. QUI OB LOCI ARITUDINEM, PLUSCULIS POLIPHILI LACHRYMUL. REPULULESCERE NEQUIT. AT SI ME FLORERE VIDERES, EXIMIA PICTURA UNIVERSIS DECORITER PRAESTARE CONSPICERES PHOEBE INQUIENS, QUEM INTACTUM URORE RELIQUE RAS, UMBRA CECIDIT. HEU POLIPHILE DESINE. FLOS SIC EXSICCATUS, NUNQUAM REVIVISCIT. VALE.

The text on this leaf is Latin, not Italian. It is given here in the original with an English rendering beneath, since the Latin is itself quotable and the first three lines carry an acrostic.

F œlix Polia, quæ sepulta vivis
C haro Marte Poliphilus quiescens
I am fecit vigilare te sopitam.

Happy Polia, who being buried yet live: Poliphilo, resting from dear Mars, has now made you, who were lulled asleep, keep watch.

The initials set out from the margin spell F · C · I.

VIATOR FAC QUAESO MORULAM, POLIAE NYMPHAE HIC EST MYROPOLIUM.
QUAE NAM INQUIES POLIA? FLOS ILLE OMNEM REDOLENS VIRTUTEM SPECTATISSIMUS.
QUI OB LOCI ARITUDINEM, PLUSCULIS POLIPHILI LACHRYMUL[IS] REPULULESCERE NEQUIT.
AT SI ME FLORERE VIDERES, EXIMIA PICTURA UNIVERSIS DECORITER PRAESTARE
CONSPICERES, PHOEBE INQUIENS, QUEM INTACTUM URORE RELIQUERAS, UMBRA CECIDIT.
HEU POLIPHILE DESINE. FLOS SIC EXSICCATUS, NUNQUAM REVIVISCIT. VALE.

Traveller, make a little pause, I beg you: here is the perfume-shop of the nymph Polia. And what, you will say, is Polia? That most admired flower, fragrant with every virtue — who, because the ground is parched, cannot put out shoots again for all Poliphilo's little tears. But if you had seen me in flower, you would have seen me surpass all others in beauty, like an exceptional painting; saying, Phoebus, the one you left untouched by your burning, a shadow has felled. Ah, Poliphilo, have done. A flower dried out like this never lives again.

Farewell.

Notes

Confidence: high. This leaf is Latin, not macaronic Italian, and it is straightforward funerary Latin at that. Two abbreviations and one line-broken word had to be expanded, all noted below and none of them doubtful.

  • MYROPOLIUM — literally an unguent- or perfume-seller's shop
  • LACHRYMUL. — the source abbreviates; expanded to lachrymulis, "little
  • RELIQUE / RAS — broken across a line in the transcription; read as
  • PHOEBE INQUIENS — vocative: the dead flower addresses the Sun, who
  • The three-line acrostic F · C · I is not the book's famous
